# PERF.md — the measured numbers, and the conditions they were measured under

Every number in this file was produced by `just bench` and `just compare` on the machine
described below, and the raw JSON is in `bench-results/`. Nothing here is estimated, extrapolated
or carried over from another machine.

**The tables below are derived, not typed.** The engine's comparison table is the median of
three full `just compare` runs, and the derivation is a checked-in script:

```bash
python3 scripts/medians.py
```

All three raw reports are committed, so the published figures can be re-derived by a reader
without re-running a benchmark. See [`bench-results/README.md`](bench-results/README.md).

**This file owns the figures.** [`STATUS.md`](STATUS.md) is the summary of where the project stands —
what is built, what is measured, what the increments found, and what is still open — and every
figure in it is the headline of a table here, checked against the same reports.

---

## The conditions

| | |
|---|---|
| **Machine** | Apple M4 Max, 14 cores, arm64 |
| **OS** | macOS 27.0.1 |
| **Toolchain** | rustc 1.97.1 (8bab26f4f 2026-07-14), Apple clang 21, release profile with fat LTO |
| **SIMD path** | NEON (AArch64) |
| **Threads** | The **headline table** is pinned to **1** for every system — the like-for-like *single-threaded* comparison. The **thread-count sweeps** (§2e cold, §2f resident, §2g load) run the same file at 1, 2, 4, 6, 8 and 14 threads, with the engine and both incumbents at the same count at every point. |
| **Dataset** | TPC-H-shaped synthetic `lineitem`, scale factor 1, **6,000,000 rows**, 242,857,841 bytes (231.6 MiB), seed 24001, null rate 0.0, **six row groups** — and, beside it, the synthetic `orders` table: **1,500,000 rows**, 49,303,490 bytes (47.0 MiB), **two row groups**, the same seed and the same scale factor. The two tables share one key space (`l_orderkey` and `o_orderkey`, 1–6,000,000), so the join has a real selectivity and not a synthetic one. |
| **Dataset provenance** | Generated by `columnar-testdata`, **not** the official TPC-H SF-1 file. See the manifest: `data/lineitem.parquet.manifest.json` |
| **Row groups** | The row-group size is a **knob**: `columnar-cli dataset --row-group-rows N`. The default is **1,000,000 rows**, so the published file still has six row groups. §3j's other two files have **twelve** (`data/lineitem-rg12.parquet`) and **twenty-four** (`data/lineitem-rg24.parquet`) row groups, the same rows and the same seed. |
| **Incumbents** | DuckDB v2.0.0 (Cyanoptera) d54cbbe3df · Polars 1.36.1 · the Rust peer, DataFusion 49.0.2 |
| **Statistics** | The **median of three full campaigns** of `just compare` and of `just sweep`, each of which takes the median of five timed iterations. The set published before this one is committed beside it as `bench-results/compare-spec024-run{1,2,3}.json`, and the code it replaced was re-measured on this host as `bench-results/compare-before-run{1,2,3}.json`, so **every** figure in this file — the old ones and the new ones — is derived from a committed report by `python3 scripts/medians.py`. §1's kernel A/B is the median of **six** campaigns of eight iterations. §1b's x86-64 set is the median of **3 to 11** campaigns per processor. The raw reports are committed in `bench-results/`, and `python3 scripts/medians.py` re-derives every table in this file from them. The spread is published where it matters. |
| **Warm/cold** | Stated per table. "Cold" means the Parquet file is read as part of the measurement. |
| **Revisions** | Every figure is the median of at least 3 campaigns. The kernel's median reproduces to within 6% across two independent campaign sets; the per-flavor spread is much wider, because this machine is shared and a run in which the whole machine stalls drags every flavor down with it. |
| **The host** | **Shared, and no quieter than the set it replaces.** The load average was **6.28–7.10** while these campaigns ran, against **4.08–7.38** for the set it replaces — and the **code's own contribution is measured against the old code on this same host** ([§2h](#2h-the-hot-paths-taken-apart)), so the two are not confused. The set before this one is committed, so the drift is **derived and published** rather than asserted. |

**This set replaced the one before it, and the machine's share of the movement is named.** The
set published before this one ran while the machine's load average was **4.08–7.38**; this one ran at
**6.28–7.10**. The six queries this
increment did not touch are the **control**, and in the cold lazy path they moved by **−5.6% to
+11.2%** between the two sets — the `scan` is at the floor, **0.1 ms** against **0.1 ms**, and is not
a percentage — while `DuckDB`'s cold `q6` moved from **31.0 ms** to **33.0 ms**
and `Polars`' from **190.1 ms** to **195.6 ms**.

**And the sort's figure moved for the code's reason, which is what this increment is about.** The
engine's own plan output says the sort's stage is **2.2 ms** of the query — and it was **5.2 ms** for
the old code on this same host, back to back: the sort's own work was a comparison made a row at a
time, and it is now made in blocks. So that the code's
share is separated from the host's, the **old code was re-measured on this same host**, back to back,
in its own three campaigns — committed as `bench-results/compare-before-run{1,2,3}.json`. The three
measurements are in [§2h](#2h-the-hot-paths-taken-apart), and the control sits beside them:
in the cold lazy path the six queries this increment did not touch moved by **0% to +6.3%** between
the two host-matched sets, while the join moved by **+0.8%** cold and **−3.2%** resident, and the
sort by **−12.7%** cold and **−51.8%** resident.

What the change does **not** do is move the comparisons: every table here measures the engine and
every incumbent at the same moment, on the same file, at the same thread count, so the *ratios* are
the machine's state divided out. And the fix this set measures is measured **back to back**, with the
two batch sizes alternating round by round, so that the machine's drift cannot bias it — see
[§3j](#3j-the-row-group-count-and-what-it-costs). Where a published number moved, both numbers are
given and the reason is named.

**Benchmark numbers are not portable between architectures.** These are AArch64 numbers. The
x86-64 / AVX2 **absolute** numbers do not exist and are not published — a GitHub-hosted runner is a
shared machine — but the x86-64 **ratios** now are, **per processor**: see
[§1b](#1b-the-kernel-on-x86-64-measured-natively). The two sets are never compared with each other.
The AVX2 path's *correctness* is verified on this machine as well: see
[AVX2 correctness](#avx2-correctness-verified-on-an-arm64-mac) below.

**This machine is shared, and it shows.** The *eager* figures spread the widest, because the eager
path reads 231 MiB and every stall in that read lands in the number; the *lazy* figures are the
tightest. DuckDB and Polars are separate processes on a busy machine, so their spreads are their
own. Where the document quotes a spread, quote it from the derivation.

**Every figure in this file is from this set**, and the comparison is like-for-like within it:
every system was measured on the same file, on the same machine, in the same three campaigns.

---

## 1. The comparison kernel

**What is measured:** one pass over a column of `f64` values, comparing every row against a
threshold and writing a bit mask. 20,000,000 rows, column `l_discount`, operator `>=`,
threshold `0.04`. **The median of six campaigns**, each the median of five iterations.

### Two baselines, not one

| flavor | what it is | rows/sec | ns/row | vs scalar | vs cpp-auto | matches |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `scalar` | the C++ reference compiled with `-fno-vectorize` | **3.23 G** | **0.309** | 1.00× | **0.61×** | 12,726,115 |
| `cpp-auto` | **the same source**, compiled with ordinary flags | **5.32 G** | **0.188** | **1.64×** | 1.00× | 12,726,115 |
| `cpp-simd` | the hand-written NEON kernel, over the C ABI | **7.77 G** | **0.129** | **2.44×** | **1.48×** | 12,726,115 |
| `rust-simd` | the Rust `std::arch` intrinsics kernel, no FFI | **8.00 G** | **0.125** | **2.46×** | **1.53×** | 12,726,115 |

**And the set this one replaces, on the same machine** — the same kernel, on the same host, measured
before the dependency moved:

| flavor | what it is | rows/sec | ns/row | vs scalar | vs cpp-auto | matches |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `scalar` | the C++ reference compiled with `-fno-vectorize` | 3.42 G | 0.292 | 1.00× | 0.59× | 12,726,115 |
| `cpp-auto` | **the same source**, compiled with ordinary flags | 5.72 G | 0.175 | 1.70× | 1.00× | 12,726,115 |
| `cpp-simd` | the hand-written NEON kernel, over the C ABI | 8.37 G | 0.120 | 2.48× | 1.47× | 12,726,115 |
| `rust-simd` | the Rust `std::arch` intrinsics kernel, no FFI | 8.29 G | 0.121 | 2.44× | 1.45× | 12,726,115 |

**The spread, because a benchmark that only publishes its best numbers is not a benchmark.** Over
the six campaigns: `scalar` 2.99–3.47 G, `cpp-auto` 3.28–5.80 G, `cpp-simd` 7.27–8.52 G,
`rust-simd` 6.96–8.59 G. This machine is not isolated, and **one campaign of the six ran with
its compiler's vectorisation starved**: that campaign's `cpp-auto` came in at 3.28 G while its
hand-written flavours ran at their usual speed, and the ratio against a starved baseline is what
stretches `cpp-simd`'s ratio against the compiler to 2.32× in that one campaign. The medians are
the numbers to quote; the range is the noise floor, and here the noise floor is the compiler's own
vectorisation.

**These numbers moved, and the reason is the host.** The kernel reads no Parquet file — it builds
its own column of twenty million values — so the dependency is not in this measurement at all.
What changed is the machine the campaigns ran on: **macOS 27.2** here, against **macOS 27.0.1** in
the set this one replaces, and **five** timed iterations per campaign where the replaced set timed
**eight**. The medians moved by up to 7% — `scalar` 3.42 G → **3.23 G**, `cpp-auto` 5.72 G →
**5.32 G**, `cpp-simd` 8.37 G → **7.77 G**, `rust-simd` 8.29 G → **8.00 G** — and the ratios
moved with them: `cpp-simd` against the compiler's vectorisation **1.47× → 1.48×**, and against
the scalar reference **2.48× → 2.44×**. The campaigns are
`bench-results/kernel-run{1,2,3,4,5,6}.json`, and the set this one replaces is
`bench-results/kernel-spec041-before-run{1,2,3,4,5,6}.json`. The old-and-new for every family is
in [§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved).

**These numbers reproduce.** An earlier session measured the same kernel on the same machine and
got `scalar` 3.51 G, `cpp-auto` 5.87 G, `cpp-simd` 8.81 G, `rust-simd` 8.77 G — within 14% of the
medians above, and with the same two ratios to within 0.1×, where the set this one replaces agreed
within 6%. Two independent sets agreeing to a few percent is a stronger claim than either set alone.

Every flavor agrees on the match count, and the differential tests compare the **masks bit for
bit**, not just the counts.

### What the two ratios mean

- **`scalar → cpp-simd` is 2.44×.** This is how much vectorisation is worth, measured against a
  reference that really is scalar. It is checked against the disassembly: the `-fno-vectorize`
  object contains **zero** vectorised loop instructions, and the ordinary object contains ~300.
- **`cpp-auto → cpp-simd` is 1.48×.** This is the one that decides whether the hand-written kernel
  earns its complexity: it beats what clang produces from the same loop by nearly half.

The first version of this table published one ratio, against a baseline that clang had
auto-vectorised. It was measuring two vectorised implementations. See
[ADR-0006](docs/adr/0006-auto-vectorised-baseline.md).

### The FFI seam

`cpp-simd` crosses a C ABI; `rust-simd` does not. They are within **3.0%** of each other — 7.77 G
against 8.00 G — and the gap is inside the campaigns' own spread (`cpp-simd` 7.27–8.52 G, `rust-simd`
6.96–8.59 G). The sign is the other way round: in the set this one replaces the C++ path was 1.0%
ahead (8.37 G against 8.29 G), and here the Rust path is ahead. **The seam is free**, and it does not
run in the C++ path's favour any more. See
[ADR-0003](docs/adr/0003-cpp-ffi-seam.md).

### AVX2 correctness (verified on an ARM64 Mac)

**This is not a performance claim.** It is the answer to "this machine has no AVX2", and the answer
turned out to matter more than expected.

The project has two SIMD paths: NEON, measured above on this machine, and AVX2, for x86-64. The
development hardware is an ARM64 MacBook, so the AVX2 half could not be *timed* here — and, when
somebody finally tried to cross-build it, it turned out it could not be *compiled* either. The
AVX2 Rust intrinsics path had never been built by anyone. It had two type errors in it (`u32`
accumulated into a `u64` mask), and it had been sitting in the tree, unchecked, for the entire life
of the project. See [ADR-0009](docs/adr/0009-avx2-without-x86-hardware.md).

The fix has two halves.

**Compilation** is now gated. `just check-x86` cross-compiles the workspace for
`x86_64-apple-darwin` on this machine, and CI runs the same check. The AVX2 path cannot rot
unnoticed again.

**Correctness** is now testable here, because Rosetta 2 executes AVX2 instructions correctly on
Apple Silicon while reporting *no* AVX2 in CPUID — the host is an ARM64 chip, so the feature bits
say so. The kernel's runtime probe therefore correctly concludes that there is no AVX2 path and
never enters it. `COLUMNAR_FORCE_SIMD=avx2` overrides the probe, which is what `just verify-avx2`
does:

```
$ just verify-avx2

-- without the override --
    forced        : auto
    simd_kind     : none
    avx2 rows     : 0
    matches oracle: true

-- with COLUMNAR_FORCE_SIMD=avx2 --
    forced        : avx2
    simd_kind     : avx2
    avx2 rows     : 1024
    matches oracle: true

RESULT: PASSED -- 3 test binaries green under Rosetta 2 with AVX2 forced.
```

The `avx2 rows` counter is the load-bearing part. A dispatch that *reports* the AVX2 path is not
proof that the path's instructions ran; the counter moves only if they did, and the mask it
produced is compared against the oracle's bit for bit. So the claim is narrow and specific: **the
AVX2 path — both the C++ FFI path and the Rust intrinsics path — compiles, executes, and produces
masks identical to the oracle's, on an ARM64 Mac.**

**What this does not establish: any AVX2 performance number.** Under Rosetta the instructions are
emulated, so a timing taken there measures the translator, not hardware. The benchmark harness
therefore *refuses to measure* while the override is in force — `just bench` exits with an
explanation rather than producing a number that means something other than what it appears to mean.
AVX2 timings require an x86-64 machine with AVX2, and are published only from one — the ratios are
in [§1b](#1b-the-kernel-on-x86-64-measured-natively), and what is still not published is in
[Not measured](#not-measured).

### 1a. The kernel in a browser

**This is a browser's number, and it is not a native number.** It is published here with everything
it depends on, and it is never compared with the native table in §1: a different runtime, a different
clock and a different compiler make the ratio between them a claim about two engines rather than
about one kernel. What the browser measures is the comparison it *can* make — the `simd128` path
against the oracle, over the same data, in the same process.

**What is measured**: one pass over a column of `f64` values, comparing every row against `0.04`
and writing a bit mask. 20,000,000 rows, five iterations per round, five rounds per campaign,
three campaigns.

| flavor | ns/row (median) | spread | rows/sec | matches |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `oracle` | **3.414** | 3.398–3.426 | 0.293 G | 12,726,115 |
| `simd128` | **0.272** | 0.267–0.278 | 3.676 G | 12,726,115 |

**`simd128` is 12.59× the oracle**, over the three campaigns (12.56×–12.64×). The published set
before this one said **3.429** and **0.273** ns/row and **12.51×**; the figures moved by less than
1%, because the browser's number is the most stable one in the repository.

| | |
|---|---|
| browser | Chromium 148.0.7778.280, Electron 42.8.0, on macOS 27.0.1 |
| engine | `wasm32-unknown-unknown`, `simd128`, release build, fat LTO, one codegen unit |
| machine | Apple M4 Max, 14 cores — **the host's load average was 8.1–8.8 throughout** |
| warm-up | one untimed pass per implementation before each timed run |
| rounds | 5 per campaign, 3 campaigns, median published with the spread |
| commit | `e866e75` |
| **the page** | **<https://oib98vz3b8zwj.space.minimax.io>** — a reader with a link opens it and watches the check run. It is the same build `just web-build` produces, and it names the commit it was built from. |

**The hosted page has no server behind it, and it says so.** It answers the report POST with a
405 and prints *"there is no report server at this address — the numbers above are this
browser's"*, because a page that claims a report was written when it was not is worse than a page
that says nothing. The published figures above are measured on the development machine, where the
host's load average is a condition of the measurement; the hosted page is the **proof**, not a
new source of published numbers.

**The spread is published.** The host's load average was between 8.1 and 8.8 while these were
taken — the machine is shared and it was busy — and the oracle's range across the campaigns is
3.398–3.426 ns/row, a 0.8% range. The `simd128` path's is 0.267–0.278, a 4.1% range, and the
**ratio**'s is 12.56×–12.64×. A browser's numbers are the least portable thing this repository
publishes, and they are published with their spread for that reason.

**The masks are identical, bit for bit, in every campaign**, and so is the **edge check**: every
operator, a NaN value, a NaN threshold, the two zeros, the two infinities and a NULL row, against
the oracle. The page makes both checks itself, before it prints anything, so a reader who opens it
runs them too.

The match count is **12,726,115**, which is the native benchmark's number: the browser rebuilds
the benchmark's column from a histogram of the file's eleven distinct discount values, in an
interleaved order that is not the file's. The browser has no Parquet reader and does not pretend to
one.

The raw reports are committed — `browser-run1.json`, `browser-run2.json`, `browser-run3.json` — and
`python3 scripts/medians.py` derives the table above from them. See
[ADR-0019](docs/adr/0019-the-kernel-in-a-browser.md).

### 1b. The kernel on x86-64, measured natively

**This is the x86-64 half of §1, and it is published as ratios only.** The absolute rows/sec and
ns/row describe a GitHub-hosted runner — a shared machine whose load this repository neither
controls nor reproduces — so they are not published. The ratios are taken from four flavors
measured in the *same process, on the same machine, in the same run*, so the machine's state
divides out of them.

**And the machine is not one machine.** GitHub's hosted runners are a **fleet**: twenty-one runs
of the same workflow, on the same commit, with the same harness, landed on six different
processors. The ratio is a property of the **processor** as much as of the kernel, so it is
published **per processor**, and the processor is named.

| processor | campaigns | `cpp-simd` vs scalar | `cpp-simd` vs `cpp-auto` | `rust-simd` vs scalar | `rust-simd` vs `cpp-auto` |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| AMD EPYC 7763 64-Core Processor | 11 | **2.23×** (1.82–2.50) | **2.24×** (1.74–2.39) | **2.02×** (1.58–2.23) | **2.02×** (1.52–2.14) |
| AMD EPYC 9V74 80-Core Processor | 4 | **2.56×** (2.14–2.78) | **2.57×** (2.14–2.78) | **2.36×** (2.12–2.53) | **2.37×** (2.12–2.54) |
| AMD EPYC 9V45 96-Core Processor | 3 | **1.69×** (1.59–1.82) | **1.68×** (1.63–1.74) | **1.58×** (1.53–1.72) | **1.57×** (1.57–1.64) |

| | |
|---|---|
| workload | one pass over 20,000,000 rows of `l_discount`, `>= 0.04`, writing a bit mask — the same kernel, the same harness and the same report as §1 |
| iterations | 8 per campaign, **3–11 campaigns** per processor |
| machine | GitHub-hosted `ubuntu-latest`, 4 cores, **1 thread**, Linux 6.17.0-1022-azure |
| toolchain | rustc 1.99.1 (58cf8f9c3 2026-10-13) |
| SIMD path | AVX2 |
| commit | `8271054` — every one of the twenty-one runs was dispatched from it |
| matches | 12,726,115 in every flavor, in every campaign, on every processor |

**The scalar baseline is the tell.** The 9V45 ran its scalar reference at **2.79 G rows/s** — twice
the 7763's 1.41 G — and it is the processor with the *smallest* AVX2 gain, 1.69× against 2.23×.
The hand-written kernel gains about as much in absolute terms on both; the *ratio* moves because
the denominator moves. A ratio published without the processor it was measured on is a number
about no machine at all, which is why the table above has a column for the processor.

**Three processors ran the campaign once each, and are not published.** They are the Intel Xeon
8370C, the Xeon 8573C and the Xeon 6973P-C, one campaign apiece, and this repository publishes
the median of at least three. One of them is the reason the rule exists: its single campaign
produced a ratio of **8.03×**, the highest number in this section — because that runner's scalar
baseline had been starved to 0.33 G rows/s, a tenth of the 9V45's. That is exactly the figure
this repository refuses to publish from one run on a machine nobody was measuring. It is named
here rather than quietly dropped, because a reader who saw it in a run's artifact should be told
why it is not in the table.

**This table is never compared with §1's.** A ratio measured on an M4 Max's NEON path and a ratio
measured on an EPYC's AVX2 path are two different measurements — different architecture,
different compiler, different machine — and setting them side by side would invite exactly the
comparison the numbers cannot support.

**What the x86-64 set does say is that the kernel is not an ARM64 story.** On three different
processors, on real x86-64 hardware, the hand-written AVX2 path is **1.7×–2.6× the scalar
reference** and **1.7×–2.6× the compiler's own vectorisation**, and the Rust `std::arch` path
lands within 10% of the C++ one. On ARM64 the two are within 3.0% of each other, the Rust one ahead; here the Rust one
is 6–10% behind, which is the FFI seam's other side — it is not free in the same way.

The twenty-one raw reports are committed — `avx2-run-<run id>.json`, one per workflow run — and
`python3 scripts/medians.py` derives the table above from them, grouped by processor. The
workflow that produced them is `bench-avx2.yml`; the correctness job ran the differential suite
on native x86-64 hardware in the same dispatch. See
[ADR-0024](docs/adr/0024-the-repository-goes-public.md).

---

## 2. The engine, against DuckDB, Polars and DataFusion

Eight queries, each measured twice where the measurement is like-for-like: **resident** (the data is
already in memory) and **cold** (the Parquet file is read as part of the measurement). All three
incumbents answer the same question in the same mode, and every answer is cross-checked against the
engine's. **The join is published in both tables now**, and the asymmetry between them is named in
[§2a](#2a-resident--the-data-is-already-in-memory): in resident mode the incumbents have both of the
join's tables in memory before the clock starts and the engine's resident path does not, so the
engine does *more* work inside its own clock than they do inside theirs.

The third incumbent is **new**: `DataFusion`, a Rust query engine reading the same Parquet through
the same Arrow stack. It is a separate cargo project under `tools/`, with its own committed lock
file, built by `just peer-build` — an **instrument, not a dependency**, so the engine's published
dependency graph does not move. See [ADR-0021](docs/adr/0021-the-materialised-column-and-a-rust-peer.md).

The queries:

| name | what it is |
|---|---|
| `scan` | `SELECT count(*)` over every row |
| `filter-numeric` | one numeric predicate, `l_discount >= 0.04` |
| `filter-string` | one predicate on the dictionary-encoded `l_shipdate` |
| `filter-range` | two numeric predicates `AND`ed, and a `SUM(l_quantity)` |
| `q6` | the TPC-H Q6 **predicate** set: two numeric and two date predicates, `AND`ed, with `count(*)` |
| `q6-revenue` | **TPC-H Q6 itself**: `sum(l_extendedprice * (1 - l_discount))` over a tighter predicate |
| `join-orders` | `lineitem` joined to `orders` on the order key, with a date range on the build side, aggregated over the whole join |
| `sort-topk` | the ten largest `l_extendedprice` values, aggregated over the sorted rows |

**`q6` and `q6-revenue` are both "Q6", and they are different queries.** `q6` is Q6's *filter*, and
it is what the repository measured for three increments. `q6-revenue` is Q6's *aggregate* — an
arithmetic expression, summed — and it is the query TPC-H actually asks. Both are published, so the
cost of the arithmetic is a **difference between two measured numbers** rather than an assertion.

**The last two are the ones this increment added**, and they are the first published queries that
are not a scan of one table. `join-orders` is an **inner equi-join, executed as a hash join**: the
build side is the smaller table — `orders`, 1,500,000 rows, of which **216,345** survive the date
range — and the probe side is `lineitem`, which stays on the lazy path with its predicates pushed
into the scan. `sort-topk` is a sort with a limit, and a limit makes it a **bounded heap of ten
rows** rather than a sort of six million. The plan output says which is which: it names the join's
key columns and the build side's file, and the sort's key and its limit. The incumbents get the
second table as well — `DuckDB` reads both files by path in cold mode, `Polars` and the
`DataFusion` peer register both tables under their names in resident mode — because a comparison
where the engine joins and the incumbent does not is not a comparison. See
[ADR-0027](docs/adr/0027-the-engine-joins-and-sorts.md).

**And the two of them are the ones this increment took apart.** The join's probe and the sort's
bounded heap were decomposed by a committed instrument, measured, and rewritten; the decomposition
and the code's share of the tables below are in
[§2h](#2h-the-hot-paths-taken-apart). See
[ADR-0028](docs/adr/0028-the-hot-paths-taken-apart.md).

was published as a loss; it is a win now.

**The sort was a loss, and it is a win now.** The engine's
resident `sort-topk` is **2.6 ms** — the median of a **2.6–2.6 ms** range across the three
campaigns — against `DuckDB`'s **3.0 ms** and the `DataFusion` peer's **3.7 ms**: the engine is
**1.2× ahead of DuckDB** and **1.4× ahead of the peer** on this query, and it was **1.9× behind
DuckDB** in the one before that. And it is **483× ahead of `Polars`**, whose **1254.6 ms**
(1242.0–1277.6 ms) is the
price of sorting six million rows where the engine keeps a heap of ten — it was **1564.0 ms**
(1381.7–1567.8 ms) in the set this file replaces — the plan says so: `sort: l_extendedprice DESC
LIMIT 10 (10 rows kept)`. The same query is published
cold in [§2b](#2b-cold--read-the-parquet-file-and-answer-in-one-go), where the engine is **2.2 ms
behind `DuckDB`** — **39.2 ms** against **37.0 ms** — and behind the peer by the same **2.2 ms**
(**37.0 ms**), and the engine is **33× ahead of `Polars`** as well. **And that cold gap is the
code's**: the old code's cold `sort-topk` is **36.99 ms** in the same session, and the reason is
named in [§3ah](#3ah-the-default-moved).

**The join is in this table now, and the asymmetry is named.** In resident mode the incumbents have
**both** tables in memory before the clock starts — `DuckDB` has loaded the `orders` file into a temp
table, `Polars` and the peer have registered both relations — while the engine's resident path
**reads the build side inside the clock**: the join's build side is a file, and the engine does not
hold it in memory before the query. The engine therefore does **more** work inside its clock than the
incumbents do inside theirs. Spec 020 withheld the figure for exactly this reason; it is published
now because the asymmetry **works against the engine** and the engine wins anyway — **24.0 ms**
(23.8–24.5 ms) against `DuckDB`'s **34.0 ms** (34.0–35.0 ms), the peer's **81.1 ms** (74.3–82.4 ms)
and `Polars`' **73.2 ms** (66.8–78.0 ms). A figure that cannot be made fairly is named, not
published; a figure that can be made *unfairly in the engine's favour* is published with the
unfairness written down beside it.

**What moved, and why.** **This table is the set the file publishes**, and the campaign behind it was
**taken again**: the reader's default was a row limit of **131,072 rows** and it is a **byte budget of
16 MiB** now, so the batch the reader holds moved with it, and every figure the batch size touches
moved with it — [§3ah](#3ah-the-default-moved) publishes them **old and new**. **And the resident
path is the one place the default cannot be: the data is already in memory, so Arrow never decodes a
file here and the reader's batch is not on the path.** The movement between the set this file
replaces and this one is the **host's** and the engine's own variance, and the incumbents — which
the default cannot have touched — moved with it: `Polars`' resident `join-orders` moved from
**90.6 ms** to **73.2 ms** and its `q6` from **75.8 ms** to **67.5 ms**, while `DuckDB`'s
`join-orders` moved from **40.0 ms** to **34.0 ms** and its `q6` from **48.0 ms** to **43.0 ms**. The
engine's own resident `join-orders` fell from **26.0 ms** to **24.0 ms** and its `q6` fell from
**10.1 ms** to **9.3 ms**. **And the interleaved set says the movement is inside the noise**: the
engine's own resident `q6` is **9.06 ms** in the old code's set and **9.33 ms** in the new one, and
`DuckDB`'s is **44.0 ms** against **43.0 ms**. **The whole of the old-and-new, for every family the bump moved, is in
[§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved)** — and the bump's own figures are the ones where the
file is read.

**1.68 ms** ([§3ah](#3ah-the-default-moved)).

**Both are published as they are.** A hash join's build side has to be in memory before the probe
side can be looked up, so the engine reads the `orders` file whole before it probes anything — and
the incumbents do the same thing with their own build sides, which is why the **cold** numbers are
the ones that can be compared at all. The join is **1.2 ms ahead** of DuckDB in this set, and the sort
is **2.2 ms behind** it; neither is hidden and neither is explained away: the numbers, the spreads and the
conditions are in the table above, **the code's share of them is measured against the old code on this
same host** in [§2h](#2h-the-hot-paths-taken-apart), and the defects this increment found are in
[§4](#4-the-defects-the-increments-found). See
[ADR-0028](docs/adr/0028-the-hot-paths-taken-apart.md).

### 2b-bis. The eager path got faster too, and it is measured

Spec 001 recorded the eager path's cold `q6` at **1921 ms**. In the measurement set that followed
it was **1181 ms** — 1.6× faster — and the cause was not the machine being quieter. It is a defect
that spec 002 fixed on the way to building something else. (The eager path's figure in the
current set is **528.1 ms**, where the set this file replaces had **587.2 ms**, the set the bump
replaces had **552.3 ms** and the set before that
had **564.6 ms**, and it moved again when
spec 008 parallelised the decode — see the
load table in §2d and [§2g](#2g-the-load-sweep--where-the-engines-biggest-number-was).)

Both paths share one decode function. That function used to copy a dictionary column's distinct
values out of *every batch* to merge them, when the merge is a lookup and needs no copy. The lazy
path never paid it: it does not materialise the dictionary column at all — the pushed predicate is
evaluated on Arrow's array directly, and the plan reports `materialised columns: (none)` — so the
copy never happened there. The eager path pays it in full: it materialises all sixteen columns,
its reader hands the file over in 5,860 batches, and every one of them copied 2,500 distinct
strings. About 14.6 million string allocations, to build a dictionary of 2,500 entries.

Putting the copy back, deliberately, and re-measuring, gives the numbers rather than the story:

| | eager `q6` cold | lazy `q6` cold |
|---|---|---|
| with the per-batch copy | **1889.9 ms** | 119.0 ms |
| without it | **1181.1 ms** | 121.5 ms |

The eager path is 1.60× faster. The lazy path does not move — 119.0 against 121.5 ms is inside
its 2.6% spread — which is the control that makes the attribution credible: the fix helps the
path that copied and leaves the path that did not.

Both columns are from the measurement set that preceded the pushdown rewrite, and they are the
figures that set was made of. The eager path's current published figure is 528.1 ms, where it was
587.2 ms in the set this file replaces, and the lazy path's is 16.6 ms, where it was 14.9 ms in that
set. The lazy path moved because
spec 003 rewrote the pushdown, and again when
spec 016 aligned the reader's batch size with the file's row groups; the eager path moved twice
more since — the dictionary fix above, and then spec 008's parallel decode, which is
[§2g](#2g-the-load-sweep--where-the-engines-biggest-number-was). The eager path's own movement
since the set the bump replaces — **552.3 ms** to **528.1 ms**, **−4.4%** — is the **default's** this
time, and not the host's: the reader's default was a row limit of **131,072 rows** and it is a **byte
budget of 16 MiB** now, so the batch the reader holds moved with it, and the campaign was taken again
because the batch size touches the figure. **And the movement the set this file replaces made was
the host's**: from **552.3 ms** to **587.2 ms**, **+6.3%**, with the eager path *being* the decode,
and the decode moving with the host — the **load** figure read **577.4 ms** against the set the bump
replaces' **542.6 ms**, **+6.4%** — and the incumbents, which do not read the file through our
Arrow, moving with it: `DuckDB`'s cold `q6` went **32.0 ms** to **35.0 ms** and the peer's **34.8 ms**
to **38.5 ms**. **The bump's own figure is the one it cannot move by the host's hand**: the lazy
path's cold `q6`, which reads the
file through the new Arrow and is **16.6 ms** against the set the bump replaces' **15.4 ms**. The
whole old-and-new is in [§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved).

The perturbed run is committed as
[`bench-results/experiment-dictionary-copy.json`](bench-results/experiment-dictionary-copy.json),
and it is **not** one of the three reports the published table is derived from.

### 2c. The lazy scan, and what it reads

The lazy path is the change spec 002 made. It does three things:

1. **It resolves the plan against the file's schema**, before reading anything, so an unknown
   column or a type mismatch is an error before a single row is decoded.
2. **It reads only the columns the plan needs.** The reader is handed a projection over the file's
   root columns; the bytes of the others are never fetched from the file.
3. **It pushes the plan's predicates into the reader**, so the rows that fail them are dropped
   during the scan rather than after it.

For `q6` that is **two columns of sixteen**:

```
columns read: l_discount, l_shipdate
pushed down: l_discount >= 0.04 and l_discount <= 0.07,
             l_shipdate >= '1994-01-01' and l_shipdate < '1995-01-01'
```

Per the Parquet footer's own metadata, those two columns are 3.0 MiB and 9.1 MiB compressed
across the file's six row groups — **12.1 MiB of the file's 231.6 MiB**, and 2.9% of its raw
bytes. `l_comment` alone, which the eager path decodes and throws away, is 50% of the raw bytes.

**The gap against DuckDB is not pruning. It is threads.** This was published as a pruning gap
once, and that was wrong; here is DuckDB's own plan for the same query on the same file:

```
╭─ Table Scan ───────────────────────────────╮
│ Function: READ_PARQUET                      │
│ Filters: (l_discount >= 0.04) AND ...      │
│ Row Groups Scanned: 6 / 6                   │
│ 315,203 rows                               │
╰─────────────────────────────────────────────╯
Data Read: 12.3 MB
```

**6 / 6 row groups scanned, 12.3 MB read** — the same two columns, the same bytes the engine
reads. DuckDB prunes *nothing* here, because there is nothing to prune with: the file has no page
index. The generator writes `EnabledStatistics::Chunk`, so there are no per-page statistics, and
neither system can skip a page it has never been told about. The engine fetches every page of the
predicate's column because the `ArrowPredicate` path evaluates over the decoded batch — but so
does DuckDB, because there is no page index to select against.

What is left is throughput, and it decomposes cleanly:

| | engine | duckdb | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Reader speed**, same job: decode all 16 columns into memory | **554.3 ms** | 1367.0 ms | **2.47× — the engine is ahead** |
| **Architecture**: read 2 columns and push the predicate down | **15.3 ms** | 33.0 ms | **0.46× — the engine is ahead** |

**Both terms now favour the engine.** The reader-speed term was **1.58×** in DuckDB's favour when
ADR-0008 recorded it; the shared decode path and the dictionary fix closed it; spec 008's parallel
eager decode took it the other way, and the engine's full-file decode is now **2.5× faster** than
DuckDB's. The architecture term was the other way round — it was **2.6×** in DuckDB's favour until
spec 009, and the engine's lazy scan of two columns is now **2.2× faster** than DuckDB's.

That is what the decomposition in [ADR-0015](docs/adr/0015-the-engine-keeps-the-mask.md) found.
The architecture term was never about the architecture. It was about a **row-range list**: the
pushdown's mask was handed to the reader as a row filter, parquet-rs turned it into a list of
2,775,138 ranges for 2,178,861 surviving rows, and the reader applied that list one range at a
time. Reading one column through the list cost **50.0 ms** against **7.8 ms** without it. The
engine now keeps the mask — one bit per row — and applies it itself.

| | duckdb, one thread | duckdb, fourteen threads |
|---|---|---|
| `q6` cold | **34.0 ms** | **10.0 ms** |

**DuckDB gets 3.4× from fourteen cores on exactly this work**, and the engine was single-threaded
by design, in this increment, because the comparison pins both incumbents to one thread to make
the comparison like-for-like. That pinning was the reason for the gap, and it was a decision this
repository made on purpose.

**Spec 005 closed most of that gap.** The engine's read is now parallel over the file's row
groups, and it preserves the file's row order exactly, so the answer does not depend on the
scheduler. §2e is the measurement. The one-thread gap was 2.1×; at six workers it was **2.5×**.

**Spec 009 closed the rest of it.** The engine's lazy scan is now **15.3 ms** at one thread against
DuckDB's **32.0 ms**, and **4.0 ms** against DuckDB's **10.0 ms** at six workers. The engine is ahead at
every thread count in §2e.

The residue that is left is the engine's own plan output, and it is small: the pushdown costs
7.3 ms of the scan — measured on the set before this one, where the scan was 24.5 ms — and that
7.3 ms is the comparison over 12,000,000 rows — twice the
rows it used to be evaluated over, because the second predicate's column is no longer narrowed by
the first. That is the price of not building the range list, and it is paid in full view.

### 2d. Where the cold time goes

| system | load (file → queryable) | subprocess wall clock |
|---|---|---|
| **engine** (eager, `Dataset::read_parquet`) | **577.4 ms** | — (a resident process) |
| **engine** (lazy, `ParquetSource::open`) | **0.1 ms** | — (a resident process) |
| duckdb (`CREATE TEMP TABLE ... AS SELECT *`) | 1285.0 ms | 57.2 ms |
| polars (`pl.read_parquet`) | 1068.5 ms | 302.6 ms |
| the Rust peer (`DataFusion`, reading the file where it lies) | 363.8 ms | 45.6 ms |

Several things are worth separating here.

**The load column is the `q6` query's load for every system** — the file into memory, before the
query's own work — and the wall clock is that same query's **cold subprocess**, timed around the
whole program. The engine's lazy row is the one exception, and it is the reader's *open*.

**The lazy path's "load" is the footer.** 0.1 ms: the file's metadata, and nothing else. The data
is read during the query, which is why the lazy path has no resident form.

**The eager path's decode is the whole file, and it still decodes too much.** 231.6 MiB in 0.577 s
is about 401 MiB/s. That is the `arrow-rs` Parquet reader going through every column of every row
group and materialising all sixteen columns into the engine's own chunk format, eagerly, whether
the query needs them or not. Spec 016 gave the eager path the same derived batch rule the lazy
path got — the batch is now the largest divisor of the row group's row count that fits under the
limit, so no row group ends with a partial batch — and spec 008 parallelised the decode;
[§2b-bis](#2b-bis-the-eager-path-got-faster-too-and-it-is-measured) has that argument and the
figures behind it. **What the eager path costs now is 577.4 ms, where the set the bump replaces
had 542.6 ms**, and that movement is the **host's** rather than the bump's: the incumbents, which
do not read the file through this repository's Arrow, moved with it — `DuckDB`'s cold `q6` from
**32.0 ms** to **35.0 ms**, the peer's from **34.8 ms** to **38.5 ms**. The figure has moved with
the machine's state since: **525.3 ms** in the one before that, **555.3 ms** in the set before that
one, **542.6 ms** in the set the bump replaces and **577.4 ms** in this one.

**And the chain this file carried before the bump was a different measurement, so it is rebuilt.**
It read the `scan` query's eager load — **844.6 ms** four sets ago, **575.5 ms** three sets ago,
**541.6 ms** two sets ago, **554.3 ms** in the set this one replaces and **555.7 ms** in this one —
and **two of those five are in no committed report at all**. The three that are derivable are the
`scan` loads in `compare-spec021`, `compare-spec023` and `compare-spec024`, and they measure a
different entry from the one this table publishes, so they are not this chain's entries either. The
chain above is the `q6` load of the committed sets — **525.3 ms**, **555.3 ms**, **542.6 ms** and
**577.4 ms** — and it carries one measurement only. The whole old-and-new is in
[§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved), and the sweep's own measurement of the same work is
in [§2g](#2g-the-load-sweep--where-the-engines-biggest-number-was).

**The load sweep measures the same work a different way, and it moved the other way.** Its
one-thread figure for the engine's load is **546.8 ms**, where the set the bump replaces had
**576.8 ms** — down, while the comparison harness's eager load above went up. The two are not the
same measurement, and this file publishes both rather than picking one: the comparison harness
measures the eager load *inside a battery of eight queries*, in one process at one thread, and the
load sweep measures the load *alone*, one number per thread count, with the query's own work
reported beside it. They agree to **6%** in this set — 546.8 ms against 577.4 ms — and they
disagree on the direction between the two sets, which is what two measurements on a shared machine
do.

**The incumbents are pinned to one thread in the headline table.** `Polars` reads the file in
1068.5 ms single-threaded, and the load sweep puts its fourteen-thread figure at about 180 ms.
That **5.9×** is the price of comparing a one-thread engine against a one-thread engine. It is the
right price for the *single-threaded* comparison, but it belongs in the reader's column, not the
query's — and §2e is where the other thread counts live. The other two loads moved the same way as
DuckDB's: `Polars`' was **1008.0 ms** and is **1068.5 ms**, and the `DataFusion` peer's was
**333.4 ms** and is **363.8 ms**, and DuckDB's own was **1222.0 ms** and is **1285.0 ms** — and the
engine's own load moved with them, from **542.6 ms** to **577.4 ms**.

**The subprocess wall clock is not the query.** It is the tool's own overhead, outside its load and
query timers: `DuckDB`'s command line client costs about **22 ms**, and `Polars`' Python interpreter
plus the `polars` import about **100 ms**, which is more than most of the query figures in §2b's
cold table. Both are excluded from the query figures — they come from the tools' own timers — and
both are reported here.

**And this column was re-derived, because the figures it carried were not in any report.** The
column is the `q6` query's **cold subprocess**, timed around the whole subprocess — the reports'
own `elapsed_ms`, which is the harness's clock, against the tools' own `query_ms`. The figures it
carried before that re-derivation were in no committed report: the same measurement over the one
before that set is **50.8 ms**, **277.2 ms** and **41.4 ms**, over the set before that one
**54.0 ms**, **285.5 ms** and **42.9 ms**, over the set the bump replaces **51.7 ms**, **281.5 ms**
and **40.9 ms**, and the figures in the table above are **57.2 ms**, **302.6 ms** and **45.6 ms**.
The rule — the `q6` query's cold `elapsed_ms` — is the one the first published set used, and it is
the one the guard reads the reports by now.

### 2e. The thread-count sweep

The headline table in §2b pins every system to **one thread**, because that is the like-for-like
*single-threaded* comparison and it is the claim that has been in this file since the first set of
numbers. It is not the only table, because a one-thread table on its own is a misleading picture
of what a user sees: `DuckDB` uses every core by default, so a user running `DuckDB` gets
10.0 ms on `q6` cold, not 34.0 ms.

So the same file is swept. **The engine and every incumbent run at the same thread count at every
point**, on the same machine, in the same harness, and the answers are cross-checked at every
point — a sweep of systems computing different answers would be a sweep of nothing.

**Two worker counts, and they are not the same number.** The **read's** parallelism is capped by
the file's **row groups**, and the **operators'** by the row count, so a table that published one
number under the header *"the engine's workers"* was publishing whichever of the two it happened
to measure. The report carries both now, and this table names them apart.

| threads | the read's workers | the filter's workers | **engine** (lazy) | engine's scan stage | engine's operators | duckdb | polars | datafusion |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 1 | **13.2 ms** *(was 14.6 ms)* | 13.2 ms *(was 14.6 ms)* | 0.0 ms | 29.0 ms *(was 33.0 ms)* | 179.2 ms *(was 197.2 ms)* | 32.2 ms *(was 35.4 ms)* |
| 2 | 2 | 1 | **6.7 ms** *(was 7.5 ms)* | 6.7 ms *(was 7.5 ms)* | 0.0 ms | 18.0 ms *(was 21.0 ms)* | 99.9 ms *(was 99.1 ms)* | 23.7 ms *(was 27.7 ms)* |
| 4 | 4 | 1 | **4.9 ms** *(was 5.4 ms)* | 4.9 ms *(was 5.4 ms)* | 0.0 ms | 13.0 ms *(was 14.0 ms)* | 65.5 ms *(was 66.6 ms)* | 14.2 ms *(was 16.6 ms)* |
| 6 | 6 | 1 | **3.9 ms** *(was 4.1 ms)* | 3.9 ms *(was 4.1 ms)* | 0.0 ms | 9.0 ms *(was 10.0 ms)* | 35.2 ms *(was 37.2 ms)* | 15.0 ms *(was 16.4 ms)* |
| 8 | 6 | 1 | **3.9 ms** *(was 4.3 ms)* | 3.9 ms *(was 4.3 ms)* | 0.0 ms | 9.0 ms *(was 10.0 ms)* | 34.5 ms *(was 36.5 ms)* | 10.6 ms *(was 11.6 ms)* |
| 14 | 6 | 1 | **3.9 ms** *(was 4.0 ms)* | 3.9 ms *(was 4.0 ms)* | 0.0 ms | 9.0 ms *(was 10.0 ms)* | 34.9 ms *(was 37.4 ms)* | 10.9 ms *(was 12.0 ms)* |

Every figure is the median of three full sweeps, each of which takes the median of three timed
iterations. The raw reports are `bench-results/scaling-run{1,2,3}.json`, and
`python3 scripts/medians.py` re-derives this table from them.

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows** — the same code, on `parquet` 54.3.1, on the
dataset the bump replaces:

| threads | the read's workers | **engine** (lazy) | engine's scan stage | engine's operators | duckdb | polars | datafusion |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | **15.4 ms** | 15.4 ms | 0.0 ms | 33.0 ms | 193.6 ms | 37.4 ms |
| 2 | 2 | **8.2 ms** | 8.1 ms | 0.0 ms | 20.0 ms | 104.5 ms | 28.0 ms |
| 4 | 4 | **6.1 ms** | 6.1 ms | 0.0 ms | 14.0 ms | 67.8 ms | 17.8 ms |
| 6 | 6 | **4.0 ms** | 4.0 ms | 0.0 ms | 10.0 ms | 41.0 ms | 17.3 ms |
| 8 | 6 | **4.7 ms** | 4.7 ms | 0.0 ms | 10.0 ms | 39.7 ms | 12.3 ms |
| 14 | 6 | **4.1 ms** | 4.1 ms | 0.0 ms | 10.0 ms | 39.8 ms | 12.1 ms |

The report that set carries no operators' column — that is the whole of
[ADR-0051](docs/adr/0051-the-workers-column-that-changed-its-meaning.md): the column was changed
from the operators' count to the read's at spec 020, and the comment that said so was left behind.

**The engine gets 3.40× from six workers, against DuckDB's 3.22×, and it is ahead of DuckDB at every
thread count.** The engine's curve is steeper than DuckDB's because the parallel part is a larger
fraction of its work: the engine's query is almost entirely *scan*, while DuckDB's is a scan plus
a parallel filter over 315,203 surviving rows.

**These figures moved, and the reason is the bump, the host and the default together.** The engine's
one-thread figure was **15.4 ms** in the set the bump replaces and is **13.2 ms** here, where it was
**14.6 ms** in the set this file replaces; DuckDB's was 33.0 ms and is **29.0 ms**, where it was
33.0 ms, Polars' 193.6 ms and is **179.2 ms**, where it was 197.2 ms, the peer's 37.4 ms and is
**32.2 ms**, where it was 35.4 ms. **The
engine's read is `parquet`'s reader**, and 58.4.1 is a different reader from 54.3.1, so the engine's
figure is the one the bump touches directly; and the reader's default was a row limit of **131,072
rows** and is a **byte budget of 16 MiB** now, so the campaign was taken again because the batch
size touches the figure. The host moved too — the machine's own floor, measured in the same
campaign, went from 6.147 ms to 4.735 ms, where it was 4.948 ms in the set this file replaces
([§3q](#3q-the-drifts-own-parts-and-what-is-left-over)) — and the peers' figures moved **down**
around it: DuckDB's from **33.0 ms** to **29.0 ms**. **The comparison is what holds**, and it holds: the engine
is ahead of DuckDB at every thread count in both sets.

**These figures moved again before this one, and every set is published.** The sweep was re-run so
that the same campaign would carry the `DataFusion` peer and the row-group
files beside it — a comparison between two campaigns taken days apart on a shared machine measures
the machine as much as the engines. The set before that was measured while
another tenant's work had the machine's memory under pressure, so every system in it is about a third
slower than the pre-fix set, and the engine's one-thread figure in it is the 24.5 ms below rather than the
19.7 ms of the pre-fix set:

| threads | engine, before | engine, the third set | duckdb, before | duckdb, the third set | polars, before | polars, the third set |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 19.7 ms | 24.5 ms | 32.0 ms | 51.0 ms | 189.4 ms | 348.3 ms |
| 2 | 9.9 ms | 14.5 ms | 21.0 ms | 36.0 ms | 101.9 ms | 191.1 ms |
| 4 | 7.1 ms | 11.5 ms | 14.0 ms | 29.0 ms | 67.7 ms | 138.6 ms |
| 6 | 4.0 ms | 7.4 ms | 10.0 ms | 19.0 ms | 39.0 ms | 76.2 ms |
| 8 | 4.2 ms | 7.3 ms | 10.0 ms | 19.0 ms | 39.0 ms | 75.2 ms |
| 14 | 4.0 ms | 7.3 ms | 10.0 ms | 19.0 ms | 38.2 ms | 68.8 ms |

**The drift in that set is the same direction in every system and by roughly the
same factor**, which is what a quieter host looks like and not what a change looks like: DuckDB's
one-thread figure fell by 35% and Polars' by 44%, and the engine's by 37%. DuckDB's figures are
back where they were two sets ago — 33.0 ms against 32.0 ms at one thread — which is the
clearest sign that the machine, and not the engine, is what moved.

**The engine's own improvement is not in this table, for the same reason.** The cost the fix
removed was *per row group*, and this file has six row groups; the fix shows up in the
**row-group files of §3j**, which have twelve and twenty-four of them, and in the back-to-back
measurement of the two batch sizes — [§3j](#3j-the-row-group-count-and-what-it-costs).

**The `DataFusion` peer is in every sweep in this file now.** It joined the sweep with the
increment before this one and had no "before" column then, because it was not in the harness when
that sweep was measured. This campaign carried it through the `q6-revenue` sweep below, the
resident sweep in §2f and the load sweep in §2g as well. The peer's row in §2b is the comparison it
was built for.

**The engine's parallelism is capped by the file, not by the machine.** The read splits the file's
**row groups** across the workers, and this file has **six** of them — so six workers is the most
that can run, and the curve is flat from six threads on. The report says so explicitly: it
records the thread count *asked for* and the workers that *ran*, side by side.

**A file written with *smaller* row groups would go further — and that is a property of the file
rather than of the engine.** This sentence used to say *larger*, and it was backwards: the read
splits the **row groups**, so the number of workers is bounded by the number of them,
`min(threads, row_groups)`. A file with more, smaller row groups has more of them and goes
further; a file with fewer, larger ones goes less far. The knob now exists, and the claim has been
measured on files with **twelve** and **twenty-four** row groups — see [§3j](#3j-the-row-group-count-and-what-it-costs).

**The operators' share of this table is 0.0 ms, and that is not a defect.** The `q6` predicate set
is **fully pushed down** into the scan, so the engine's own filter has nothing left to do — the
operators are in the scan stage, and the filter's own worker count is **one** at every thread count
in this table, because there is nothing for it to do. The query where they are not pushed down is
`q6-revenue`, below, and where they are the *whole* of the work is
[§2f](#2f-the-resident-sweep-where-the-operators-are-the-measurement).

**The rows come back in the file's order, whatever the thread count.** `SUM` is not associative,
so a parallel read that ordered its rows by *completion* would make the answer depend on the
scheduler. The parallel read is bit-identical to the single-threaded one, and that is checked
rather than asserted — see [ADR-0011](docs/adr/0011-parallel-decode.md).

**The one-thread row in this table (13.2 ms) is the one-thread row in §2b (16.6 ms), and they no
longer agree within their spreads.** They used to differ by 1%, because the comparison campaign and the
sweep are two campaigns on a shared machine. Both campaigns were re-run in this set, and the two
now differ by 20%, 13.2 ms against 16.6 ms — and the sweep's own range is 12.4–13.4 ms, so the
other figure is outside it. Both are published,
because a reader who compared the two would otherwise have to wonder which one to believe. The two
are the same measurement of the same thing — the lazy path's cold `q6` — from two campaigns on a
shared machine, and the distance between them is what a shared machine is worth.

### The same sweep, on the real Q6

The real Q6 reads `l_extendedprice` — the biggest column in the file — so its scan is heavier
than `q6`'s, and the sweep is worth publishing for it separately.

| threads | the read's workers | the filter's workers | **engine** (lazy) | engine's scan stage | engine's operators | duckdb | polars | datafusion |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 1 | **49.4 ms** *(was 58.9 ms)* | 48.1 ms *(was 57.2 ms)* | 1.4 ms *(was 1.5 ms)* | 64.0 ms *(was 70.0 ms)* | 185.9 ms *(was 206.5 ms)* | 103.8 ms *(was 115.3 ms)* |
| 2 | 2 | 2 | **26.4 ms** *(was 29.4 ms)* | 25.2 ms *(was 28.0 ms)* | 1.2 ms *(was 1.5 ms)* | 36.0 ms *(was 39.0 ms)* | 103.3 ms *(was 106.8 ms)* | 72.2 ms *(was 79.8 ms)* |
| 4 | 4 | 4 | **19.5 ms** *(was 21.2 ms)* | 18.4 ms *(was 19.8 ms)* | 1.1 ms *(was 1.3 ms)* | 25.0 ms *(was 27.0 ms)* | 68.7 ms *(was 72.4 ms)* | 40.3 ms *(was 43.6 ms)* |
| 6 | 6 | 6 | **11.8 ms** *(was 13.5 ms)* | 10.6 ms *(was 12.1 ms)* | 1.2 ms *(was 1.4 ms)* | 16.0 ms *(was 18.0 ms)* | 38.0 ms *(was 43.1 ms)* | 41.5 ms *(was 44.8 ms)* |
| 8 | 6 | 8 | **12.0 ms** *(was 13.8 ms)* | 10.7 ms *(was 12.4 ms)* | 1.3 ms *(was 1.5 ms)* | 16.0 ms *(was 18.0 ms)* | 36.7 ms *(was 40.8 ms)* | 25.5 ms *(was 28.5 ms)* |
| 14 | 6 | 14 | **12.6 ms** *(was 13.9 ms)* | 10.9 ms *(was 12.0 ms)* | 1.7 ms *(was 1.9 ms)* | 16.0 ms *(was 18.0 ms)* | 36.5 ms *(was 41.2 ms)* | 25.9 ms *(was 29.1 ms)* |

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows:**

| threads | the read's workers | **engine** (lazy) | engine's scan stage | engine's operators | duckdb | polars | datafusion |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | **58.4 ms** | 56.6 ms | 1.6 ms | 69.0 ms | 197.7 ms | 115.0 ms |
| 2 | 2 | **32.0 ms** | 29.9 ms | 1.4 ms | 40.0 ms | 111.7 ms | 83.6 ms |
| 4 | 4 | **22.6 ms** | 21.3 ms | 1.3 ms | 27.0 ms | 73.1 ms | 47.1 ms |
| 6 | 6 | **14.3 ms** | 12.8 ms | 1.3 ms | 19.0 ms | 44.3 ms | 46.4 ms |
| 8 | 6 | **14.6 ms** | 12.9 ms | 1.7 ms | 19.0 ms | 41.3 ms | 28.8 ms |
| 14 | 6 | **14.9 ms** | 12.8 ms | 1.8 ms | 19.0 ms | 44.8 ms | 29.3 ms |

**The engine gets 4.18× from six workers, against DuckDB's 4.00×, and it is ahead of DuckDB at every
thread count.** It is ahead of it now — 49.4 ms against 64.0 ms at one thread. The shape is the same
as `q6`'s and for the same reason: the
engine parallelises the scan, and its query is almost entirely scan.

**This sweep was re-run with the fix, and every absolute figure in it is smaller than the set
before it** — the engine's one-thread figure was 112.1 ms and is 49.4 ms, DuckDB's was 129.0 ms
and is 64.0 ms — because the host is no longer thrashing, for every system, at the same time. The
*ratios* are the claim, and they hold: the engine's speedup at fourteen threads was 4.38× and then
4.14× and is **3.93×**, DuckDB's was 3.79× and then 3.58× and is **4.00×**. See
[The conditions](#the-conditions).

**The six-thread row's spread is narrow, and that is published rather than dropped: 11.8 ms is the
median of an 11.8–12.2 ms range.** The set the bump replaces had a wide spread there — 13.9–16.0 ms,
and one run of 37.6 ms before that, on a machine that was busy with something else. The conclusion
is about the *median* either way.

**The arithmetic is not in any of these numbers.** The scan stage is 48.1 ms of the 49.4 ms
one-thread figure, and the operators — which include the arithmetic — are 1.4 ms of it, and
[§3h](#3h-the-materialised-column-and-what-it-is-made-of) takes the arithmetic out of that: it is
**1.28 ms** of the real Q6. The engine's number is the cost of reading a column, and the
arithmetic over the rows that survived the filter is close to free.

**The operators' column is nearly flat, and that is the finding.** The operators are 1.4 ms of a
49.4 ms cold query — **2.8%** — because the scan pushed two of the five predicates down and
returned 407,082 rows, and the engine's filter only had to apply the predicate on `l_discount`,
which the expression also reads and which therefore could not be pushed down. The operators'
parallelism has almost nothing to work with *in the cold path*, and the table says so rather than
leaving a reader to assume otherwise. The path where they are the query is
[§2f](#2f-the-resident-sweep-where-the-operators-are-the-measurement).

The real Q6 reads `l_extendedprice` — the biggest column in the file — so its scan is heavier

| threads | engine's workers | **engine** (lazy) | engine's scan stage | engine's operators | duckdb | polars | datafusion |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | **58.4 ms** | 56.6 ms | 1.6 ms | 69.0 ms | 197.7 ms | 115.0 ms |
| 2 | 2 | **32.0 ms** | 29.9 ms | 1.4 ms | 40.0 ms | 111.7 ms | 83.6 ms |
| 4 | 4 | **22.6 ms** | 21.3 ms | 1.3 ms | 27.0 ms | 73.1 ms | 47.1 ms |
| 6 | 6 | **14.3 ms** | 12.8 ms | 1.3 ms | 19.0 ms | 44.3 ms | 46.4 ms |
| 8 | 6 | **14.6 ms** | 12.9 ms | 1.7 ms | 19.0 ms | 41.3 ms | 28.8 ms |
| 14 | 6 | **14.9 ms** | 12.8 ms | 1.8 ms | 19.0 ms | 44.8 ms | 29.3 ms |

engine parallelises the scan, and its query is almost entirely scan.

**This sweep was re-run with the fix, and every absolute figure in it is smaller than the set
before it** — the engine's one-thread figure was 112.1 ms and is 58.4 ms, DuckDB's was 129.0 ms
and is 69.0 ms — because the host is no longer thrashing, for every system, at the same time. The

**The six-thread row's spread is wide, and it is published rather than dropped: 14.3 ms is the

**The arithmetic is not in any of these numbers.** The scan stage is 56.6 ms of the 58.4 ms

**0.85 ms** of the real Q6. The engine's number is the cost of reading a column, and the
arithmetic over the rows that survived the filter is close to free.

**The operators' column is nearly flat, and that is the finding.** The operators are 1.6 ms of a
58.4 ms cold query — **2.7%** — because the scan pushed two of the five predicates down and
returned 407,082 rows, and the engine's filter only had to apply the predicate on `l_discount`,
which the expression also reads and which therefore could not be pushed down. The operators'
parallelism has almost nothing to work with *in the cold path*, and the table says so rather than
leaving a reader to assume otherwise. The path where they are the query is
[§2f](#2f-the-resident-sweep-where-the-operators-are-the-measurement).

### 2f. The resident sweep, where the operators are the measurement

The cold sweep measures the *read* and the operators together, and the read is essentially all of
it. That is
the right measurement for a user waiting on a command line, and the wrong one for the question
"how fast is the query engine".

So the same sweep is run again in **resident** mode: the file is loaded once, outside the timed
region, and the query is timed on its own. The incumbents are measured the same way, at the same
thread count, in the same harness — the symmetry the constitution requires.

**And this is the table where the two worker counts differ.** The read's parallelism is capped by
the file's **six row groups**, and the operators' by the **row count**, so at eight and fourteen
threads the read runs **six** workers and the filter runs **eight** and **fourteen**. Both are in
the table, and they are named apart.

**`q6`, resident:**

| threads | the read's workers | the filter's workers | the projection's workers | **engine** (resident) | engine's operators | duckdb | polars | datafusion |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | **6.7 ms** | 6.6 ms | 46.0 ms | 68.6 ms | 24.5 ms |
| 2 | 2 | 2 | 1 | **3.8 ms** | 3.8 ms | 25.0 ms | 35.3 ms | 15.0 ms |
| 4 | 4 | 4 | 1 | **2.5 ms** | 2.5 ms | 13.0 ms | 18.0 ms | 9.6 ms |
| 6 | 6 | 6 | 1 | **2.1 ms** | 2.1 ms | 10.0 ms | 12.3 ms | 7.3 ms |
| 8 | 6 | **8** | 1 | **1.9 ms** | 1.9 ms | 8.0 ms | 10.2 ms | 6.4 ms |
| 14 | 6 | **14** | 1 | **1.8 ms** | 1.8 ms | 7.0 ms | 9.5 ms | 6.0 ms |

**`q6-revenue`, resident:**

| threads | the read's workers | the filter's workers | the projection's workers | **engine** (resident) | engine's operators | duckdb | polars | datafusion |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | **9.6 ms** | 9.6 ms | 51.0 ms | 70.1 ms | 62.8 ms |
| 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | **5.6 ms** | 5.5 ms | 26.0 ms | 36.4 ms | 34.1 ms |

Every figure is the median of three full sweeps of five timed iterations. The raw reports are
`bench-results/scaling-resident-run{1,2,3}.json` and
`bench-results/scaling-resident-revenue-run{1,2,3}.json`, and `python3 scripts/medians.py`
re-derives both tables from them.

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows** — the same code, on `parquet` 54.3.1, on the
dataset the bump replaces:

**`q6`, resident:**

| threads | the workers the report recorded | **engine** (resident) | engine's operators | duckdb | polars | datafusion |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 7.8 ms | 7.8 ms | 46.0 ms | 69.0 ms | 24.5 ms |
| 2 | 2 | 4.2 ms | 4.2 ms | 27.0 ms | 37.9 ms | 16.9 ms |
| 4 | 4 | 2.6 ms | 2.6 ms | 15.0 ms | 19.7 ms | 10.6 ms |
| 6 | 6 | 2.0 ms | 2.0 ms | 11.0 ms | 13.2 ms | 7.8 ms |
| 8 | **8** | 2.0 ms | 2.0 ms | 9.0 ms | 10.9 ms | 7.1 ms |
| 14 | **14** | 1.8 ms | 1.8 ms | 8.0 ms | 9.5 ms | 6.4 ms |

**`q6-revenue`, resident:**

| threads | the workers the report recorded | **engine** (resident) | engine's operators | duckdb | polars | datafusion |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 9.3 ms | 9.2 ms | 48.0 ms | 75.9 ms | 65.9 ms |
| 2 | 2 | 5.7 ms | 5.7 ms | 29.0 ms | 38.9 ms | 37.4 ms |
| 4 | 4 | 4.0 ms | 3.9 ms | 17.0 ms | 19.8 ms | 21.0 ms |
| 6 | 6 | 3.2 ms | 3.2 ms | 12.0 ms | 14.5 ms | 16.1 ms |
| 8 | **8** | 3.5 ms | 3.5 ms | 11.0 ms | 12.6 ms | 13.7 ms |
| 14 | **14** | 3.4 ms | 3.4 ms | 10.0 ms | 11.0 ms | 13.7 ms |

**That table's worker column was the operators' count, and it was published under the read's
header.** The report recorded the **filter's** workers in the resident mode, and the column's doc
comment still says so; spec 020 changed the code to record the **read's** count in every mode and
left the comment behind. **So the eight and fourteen in that table are this table's filter column,
not a different measurement** — the same number, in the same query, on the same file. The reason is
in [ADR-0051](docs/adr/0051-the-workers-column-that-changed-its-meaning.md), and the report now
carries both counts so that a reader is never left guessing which one a column holds.

**The engine is ahead of DuckDB at every thread count, and that is what the table says.** It is
**6.9× faster** at one thread (6.7 ms against 46.0 ms) and **3.9× faster** at fourteen (1.8 ms
against 7.0 ms) — against 5.9× and 4.4× in the set the bump replaces. The gap narrows, because
DuckDB's parallel filter over the survivors scales better than the engine's.

**The engine's resident query is faster in this set than in the set the bump replaces, and the
figure repeats.** Its one-thread figure was **7.8 ms** in the set the bump replaces and is
**6.7 ms** here, and the first campaign on the bumped tree measured 6.7 ms as well, so this is
not one campaign's noise. **The resident path reads no file inside the clock** — the file is
decoded before it — so the bump's contribution here is the *decode* that happens before the clock
starts, and the incumbents, which are the yardstick, did not move at all: DuckDB's one-thread
figure was 46.0 ms and is 46.0 ms, Polars' 69.0 ms and is 68.6 ms, the peer's 24.5 ms and is
24.5 ms.

**And that is why the engine's speedup fell while DuckDB's rose.** The engine's one-thread end
got faster and its fourteen-thread end did not — 1.8 ms in both sets — so its speedup went from
**4.27×** to **3.64×**; DuckDB's went from **5.75×** to **6.57×**, because its one-thread end did
not move and its fourteen-thread end did. Polars' is level, 7.3× against 7.2×. **The comparison is
what this table is for, and it holds in both sets**: the engine is ahead of DuckDB at every thread
count, by 6.9× at one thread and 3.9× at fourteen.

**The operators' worker count is not capped at six, and that is the difference from §2e.** The
read's parallelism is capped by the file's six row groups; the **operators'** is capped by the row
count, so at eight and fourteen threads the filter runs eight and fourteen workers. The table
records the workers that ran, because the requested count and the actual one now differ in a way
they did not before.

**The engine's own curve is monotone here, and the spread says why the old one was not.** On `q6`
the fourteen-thread row is 1.8 ms with a **1.8–1.9 ms** range, and the eight-thread row is 1.9 ms
with a 1.9–2.1 ms
range: the machine could give the fourteen workers a quiet run this time. The same is visible in
DuckDB's row — 8.0 ms at eight threads and 7.0 ms at fourteen — and in Polars' 10.2 ms and
9.5 ms. Every one of them is published rather than dropped, and the engine's `q6-revenue` row
shows the same shape: 3.8 ms at four threads, 3.3 ms at eight, 3.6 ms at fourteen, with spreads
of 3.7–3.9 ms, 3.1–3.5 ms and 3.5–16.1 ms. The medians are the figures, and the spread
is the evidence that the *curve* is the host's here.

**These figures are not the same as §2b's resident row, and the difference is the harness.** The
comparison harness re-reads the file inside every iteration, so each resident query runs after a
231 MiB decode has churned the allocator. The resident sweep loads the file once and then runs
the query against it. Both are published.


### 2g. The load sweep — where the engine's biggest number was

The **load** is the time to get the file into memory: the engine decodes every column into its own
chunk format, `DuckDB` into a temp table, `Polars` into a frame. It is the only measurement in
this file that is about the *file* rather than the *query*, and it was the engine's worst figure —
**1277.8 ms**, the largest number in the repository, and the one place the engine was behind both
incumbents.

So it is swept the same way as the other two: the engine and both incumbents load the same file at
the same thread counts, in the same harness.

| threads | the read's workers | **engine** (load) | duckdb (load) | polars (load) | datafusion (load) | engine vs duckdb |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | **546.8 ms** | 1228.0 ms | 1019.4 ms | 328.9 ms | **2.2× ahead** |
| 2 | 2 | 379.2 ms | 601.0 ms | 509.0 ms | 224.9 ms | 1.6× ahead |
| 4 | 4 | 292.2 ms | 410.0 ms | 343.8 ms | 120.7 ms | 1.4× ahead |
| 6 | 6 | **216.5 ms** | 223.0 ms | 190.3 ms | 119.7 ms | **1.03× — ahead** |
| 8 | 6 | 216.9 ms | 220.0 ms | 181.5 ms | 69.8 ms | 1.01× — ahead |
| 14 | 6 | 211.3 ms | 219.0 ms | 180.0 ms | 70.6 ms | 1.04× — ahead |

Every figure is the median of three full sweeps of five timed iterations. The raw reports are
`bench-results/scaling-load-run{1,2,3}.json`, and `python3 scripts/medians.py` re-derives the table
from them.

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows:**

| threads | the read's workers | **engine** (load) | duckdb (load) | polars (load) | datafusion (load) | engine vs duckdb |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | **576.8 ms** | 1275.0 ms | 1069.8 ms | 362.3 ms | **2.2× ahead** |
| 2 | 2 | 423.9 ms | 635.0 ms | 536.1 ms | 242.0 ms | 1.5× ahead |
| 4 | 4 | 312.2 ms | 423.0 ms | 372.5 ms | 128.5 ms | 1.4× ahead |
| 6 | 6 | **251.0 ms** | 239.0 ms | 193.5 ms | 127.7 ms | **0.95× — behind** |
| 8 | 6 | 246.0 ms | 245.0 ms | 200.2 ms | 76.0 ms | 1.00× level |
| 14 | 6 | 243.9 ms | 235.0 ms | 204.8 ms | 83.5 ms | 0.96× — behind |

**Every absolute figure in this table is smaller than the set it replaces, and the reason is the
host.** The engine's one-thread load was **842.3 ms** in the set before that one, **576.8 ms** in the
set the bump replaces and is **546.8 ms** here; DuckDB's was 1793.0 ms, then 1275.0 ms, and is
1228.0 ms; the engine's six-worker load was 433.5 ms, then 251.0 ms, and is 216.5 ms.
The set before them was measured on a machine under another tenant's memory pressure — see
[The conditions](#the-conditions) — and every system moved by roughly that much, the other way.
The *ratios* are what this table is about, and they hold.

**The engine goes from 0.95× DuckDB to 2.2× DuckDB at one thread.** The load was the one place
the engine was behind, and at one thread — where the comparison is like-for-like — it is now the
one place it is furthest ahead of DuckDB.

**At six threads the engine is ahead of DuckDB, 216.5 ms against 223.0 ms, where in the set the
bump replaces it was behind, 251.0 ms against 239.0 ms.** The reason is the file.
The read splits the file's
**row groups** across the workers, and this file has **six** — so six workers is the ceiling, and
the curve is flat from six threads on. `DuckDB` is not capped by the file and keeps using threads,
and it is behind by 1–4% at six, eight and fourteen threads, where in the set the bump replaces it
was ahead by 4–5%; in the set before that one the engine was 1.2× ahead at six. `Polars` is a
little ahead of the engine at every count.
The `DataFusion` peer is **faster than
everybody** on this measurement, and it is the one system in the table that does not decode the
file into the engine's own chunk format at all — it reads the file where it lies.

**Most of the win was not the parallelism.** The eager path used the Parquet reader's default
batch size of 1,024 rows, and the per-batch cost scales with the number of columns — the eager
path reads all sixteen, so it rebuilt sixteen dictionary tables 5,860 times instead of 733. In a
controlled comparison — the same binary, the same process shape, one change at a time — fixing
that constant took the one-thread load from **1145.6 ms to 587.0 ms**, and the parallel decode
took it the rest of the way to **550.2 ms** — and the one-thread load is **546.8 ms** in this set,
on a machine that is no longer under another tenant's memory pressure. The full account is in
[ADR-0014](docs/adr/0014-parallel-eager-decode.md).

**And spec 016 gave this path the same rule the lazy path got.** The eager read had the same
defect — a batch size that divided no row group's row count — and it now derives its batch from
the file the same way. The load sweep above is the only published measurement of that change on
the eager path, and it is measured on the host's conditions, so it is published as measured.


### 2h. The hot paths, taken apart

**What is measured:** the join's probe and the sort, taken apart, by an instrument committed in the
tree — `crates/columnar-engine/examples/operator_probe.rs`. Six million probe rows, a table of
**216,345** build keys, the same file, the same host, the two code versions measured **back to back**
in the same session. The reports are `bench-results/operator-probe-before.json` and
`bench-results/operator-probe-after.json`.

**Every candidate is checked against the engine's own output, row for row, before it is timed.** A
candidate that is wrong is not a candidate. The check is not a formality: it caught the sort's own
bounded heap keeping the **best** row instead of the worst, and it caught the probe's own
direct-addressed table answering for a key below its base. Both were real defects, and both are in
[§4](#4-the-defects-the-increments-found).

**The join's probe, over 6,000,000 rows against 216,345 build keys:**

| | before | after | |
|---|---|---|---|
| reading the key column, through the enum | 9.3 ms | 9.4 ms | the floor the old code paid |
| reading the key column's **own integers** | 1.7 ms | 1.7 ms | **the real floor** |
| **the engine's probe** | **102.7 ms** | **9.0 ms** | **11.4×** |
| the engine's table behind a bitmap | 95.5 ms | 100.6 ms | a bitmap does not pay |
| an open-addressed table over the key's bits | 66.2 ms | 70.0 ms | the hash table's shape |
| the same, with the Fibonacci hash | 66.6 ms | 71.0 ms | |
| …behind an 8 KiB bitmap | 68.9 ms | 70.4 ms | |
| …behind a 32 KiB bitmap | 70.0 ms | 71.3 ms | |
| a table addressed by the key itself, 4 bytes a count | 44.0 ms | 42.9 ms | |
| **a table addressed by the key itself, 1 byte a count** | **16.4 ms** | **15.5 ms** | the table's shape |

**The sort, over 6,000,000 rows, with a limit of ten:**

| | before | after | |
|---|---|---|---|
| reading the key column | 9.9 ms | 9.2 ms | the floor the old code paid |
| reading it without the validity bitmap | 3.5 ms | 3.6 ms | |
| **the engine's bounded sort** | **13.3 ms** | **3.7 ms** | **3.6×** |
| the same sort, written by hand over a plain read | 4.8 ms | 4.7 ms | |

**The engine's probe is now faster than every candidate in the table, and the reason is worth
publishing.** The candidates all read the key through the engine's `KeyValue` enum — 24 bytes wide,
because one of its variants is a string — and the enum is what they pay for: the engine's own probe
reads the column's **own integers**, with no enum and no validity bitmap, and the floor for that is
**1.7 ms**. So the probe is now **9.0 ms** against a floor of **1.7 ms**, where before it was
**102.7 ms** against a floor of **1.7 ms**.

**The sort was at the floor too — and the floor was wrong.** The engine's bounded sort measured
**3.7 ms** against a plain-read floor of **3.6 ms**, so the conclusion was that the sort's own work was
no longer worth optimising: the key read is 3.6 ms of the 3.7 ms, and the comparison and the heap are
the rest. **That floor was a scalar read** — a loop that counts the values one at a time — and the
sort was counting them one at a time too. Spec 025's instrument measures the same column **compared in
blocks**, and the comparison is **0.96 ms**: the sort's own work was **2.2 ms** of the operator's
3.2 ms, and it was the comparison, not the read. [§3n](#3n-the-sort-taken-apart) takes it apart, and
the sort now compares its keys in blocks.

**A negative result, published.** **A bitmap in front of the table does not help.** It was built and
measured at three sizes — 8 KiB, 32 KiB and 256 KiB — in front of two different tables, and it is
**never better than the table alone**: against the table's own **71.0 ms**, the 8 KiB filter reads
**70.4 ms** and the 32 KiB filter **71.3 ms**, which is the same number to within the run-to-run
noise, and the filter costs memory to hold. In the old code it was worse than noise: **68.9 ms** and
**70.0 ms** against the table's **66.6 ms**. The reason is arithmetic: with 216,345 keys a filter
needs about **six million bits** before it rejects anything at all — a filter with fewer bits than
keys is saturated, and a saturated filter rejects nothing — and a filter that big is no cheaper to
consult than the 6 MB table it guards. The direct-addressed table beats it anyway, for a different
reason: it consults nothing, so there is nothing for a filter to guard.

**And the code's share of the published tables, measured against the old code on this same host.** The
old code was re-measured here, in its own three campaigns — committed as
`bench-results/compare-before-run{1,2,3}.json` — so the host's drift is divided out:

| | the last set (load 4.08–7.38) | the set this one replaces (load 6.28–7.10) | old code, this host (load 7.59–13.77) | new code, this host (load 7.58–8.97) | the code's share |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `join-orders` cold, lazy | 89.8 ms | 96.7 ms | 97.3 ms | **97.6 ms** | **+0.3%** — the machine's |
| `join-orders` resident | 23.8 ms | 24.2 ms | 25.1 ms | **26.6 ms** | **+6.0%** — **not the machine's**, and the parts cannot say what it is |
| `sort-topk` cold, lazy | 40.1 ms | 37.2 ms | 38.2 ms | **39.5 ms** | **+3.4%** — the machine's |
| `sort-topk` resident | 5.7 ms | 2.7 ms | 3.0 ms | **2.7 ms** | **−10.0%** — the machine's |
| *`q6` cold, lazy — the control* | *14.8 ms* | *15.7 ms* | *15.5 ms* | *15.4 ms* | *−0.6%* |
| *`q6-revenue` cold, lazy — the control* | *56.1 ms* | *62.4 ms* | *59.0 ms* | *58.9 ms* | *−0.2%* |

**The control is the point.** The queries this increment did not touch moved by **−0.6% to −0.2%** in
the cold lazy path between the two host-matched sets — the host was **faster** in the later campaigns,
so every one of those movements works **against** the code, not for it. The join's cold **+0.3%** is
inside the untouched sort's **+3.4%**, so it is the machine's: the join's cold stage moved
**26.0 ms** to **26.4 ms**, inside both spreads. The join's **resident +6.0%** is the other way: the
