machine got faster, so the code's share of that movement is at least the **6.0 points** between it and
the control's best, and at most the **16.0 points** between it and the untouched sort's **−10.0%**.

**And the movement is not the machine's drift, and the parts cannot say what it is.** A same-moment
A/B — the old code and the new code, one campaign each, minutes apart on this same host, committed as
`bench-results/compare-ab-old-run1.json` and `bench-results/compare-ab-new-run1.json` — reads the old
code's **resident** join stage at **21.7 ms** and the new code's at **23.2 ms**, and the old code's
cold query at **95.0 ms** against the new code's **93.2 ms**. So the resident path really did move, and
the cold path really did not. **The parts cannot account for it**: the probe's own measurement is
**0.950 ms faster** ([§3o](#3o-the-joins-stage-taken-apart)), the **build side's read** is
**0.275 ms faster**, and the **floor** — the table's own load, which this change cannot touch — moved
**1.514 ms slower**. **This increment does not claim what the resident movement is.**

**The plan output reads it a stage at a time, and the stages are in the committed reports too.** The
engine's own account of the same work, from the same three campaigns:

| stage | the last set (load 4.08–7.38) | the set this one replaces (load 6.28–7.10) | old code, this host (load 7.59–13.77) | new code, this host (load 7.58–8.97) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `join-orders` scan | 67.4 ms | 73.6 ms | 70.5 ms | **70.2 ms** |
| `join-orders` project | 0.5 ms | 0.6 ms | 0.5 ms | **0.5 ms** |
| `join-orders` **join** | **22.6 ms** | **24.2 ms** | **26.0 ms** | **26.4 ms** |
| `sort-topk` scan | 35.2 ms | 39.0 ms | 36.2 ms | **36.1 ms** |
| `sort-topk` **sort** | **4.9 ms** | **2.2 ms** | **2.2 ms** | **2.4 ms** |

**And the project stage changed shape, not only its time.** It reports the **216,783 rows it
gathered** now, where it used to report the 6,000,000 it was handed — the defect R6 removed, and
the change spec 023 made to the scan's own rows out. The stage table above is read from the reports,
so the rows are in there too.

**The scan stages are the control for the scan.** They are Arrow's decode, which this increment did
not touch: the sort's moved from **36.2 ms** to **36.1 ms** on this same host, and the join's from
**70.5 ms** to **70.2 ms**. **Neither moved**, and that is what says the decode did not move — and it
is the control the spec names, because the scan's elapsed is the reader's own work **less** the
probe's, so a probe that got faster cancels out of it.

**The sort's own stage is unchanged, because this increment did not touch the sort.** Its stage moved
from **2.2 ms** to **2.4 ms** on this host, back to back with the old code, and the plan output's
account of the query still closes: **0.01 ms** outside the stages. The sort's own work — the
**comparison**, in blocks rather than a row at a time — is where
[§3n](#3n-the-sort-taken-apart) takes it apart, and it is not what this increment touched.

**And the resident stage is the one figure this increment cannot explain.** The **resident** join
stage is **23.6 ms** against **22.2 ms** for the old code on this same host, and the same-moment A/B
reads **23.2 ms** against **21.7 ms**. The cold stage moved **26.0 ms** to **26.4 ms**, inside both
spreads, and the cold query moved **97.3 ms** to **97.6 ms**. So the movement is in the **resident**
path alone, it is not the machine's drift, and the parts that would explain it say the opposite. It is
published as it is, and it is the next question.

**And the plan output says so, in the reports.** Every campaign records the plan output of every
query it ran, and the last line of that table is now the account:

```
scan       6000000  216783    70.2 ms
project    216783   216783     0.5 ms
join       6000000  216783    26.4 ms
aggregate  216783   1          0.2 ms
accounted: 97.39 ms of the query's 97.40 ms (0.01 ms outside the stages)
```

**The join's stage reports the rows the probe consumed**, where the report this block was taken from
reported the rows that survived it. See
[§3m](#3m-the-time-the-plan-output-does-not-name) for why the two are not about the same thing.

### 2i. The same code, measured again

**This increment changes no engine work, and the code's own figures stand.** The comparison campaigns
were re-run on the tree this increment ships, and — **back to back, in the same session** — on the
tree it replaces, which is a worktree at `79ec08f`. Three campaigns each, both trees **clean**, and
the two codes **interleaved**, so that a host which drifts moves both sides. The reports are
`bench-results/compare-spec034-run{1,2,3}.json` and
`bench-results/compare-spec034-before-run{1,2,3}.json`.

**And the standing set beside them has been re-measured, because the dependency moved.** The set the
bump replaces is committed as `bench-results/compare-spec041-before-run{1,2,3}.json`, over the file
as it stood, **242,857,841** bytes; the set this file publishes is
`bench-results/compare-run{1,2,3}.json`, over the regenerated file, **242,859,461** bytes, read by
the reader the bump brought. **Two different things are replaced in this section, and the reports
keep them apart**: that standing set, and the code this increment replaces, measured back to back
against it in the same session. The first table's three sets are three sessions — the standing set
was re-measured after the bump, the two beside it before it — and **the code's share is the
difference between the two A/B columns alone**, so the bump does not enter it. The figures the bump
moved are collected in [§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved).

**The engine's own figures, and the code this increment replaces beside them:**

| | the published set | this set | the code this increment replaces | the code's share |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `join-orders`, cold | **87.8 ms** (was 100.0 ms) | 82.0 ms | 82.5 ms | **−0.5 ms** — the machine's |
| `join-orders`, resident | **24.0 ms** *(was 26.0 ms)* | 22.0 ms | 21.8 ms | **+0.2 ms** — the machine's |
| `sort-topk`, cold | **39.2 ms** *(was 39.0 ms)* | 34.9 ms | 34.3 ms | **+0.6 ms** — the machine's |
| `sort-topk`, resident | **2.6 ms** *(was 3.1 ms)* | 2.4 ms | 2.4 ms | **0.0 ms** — the machine's |
| *`q6`, cold — the control* | *16.6 ms* *(was 14.9 ms)* | *14.0 ms* | *14.3 ms* | *−0.3 ms* |
| *`q6-revenue`, cold — the control* | *52.9 ms* *(was 59.1 ms)* | *52.9 ms* | *52.5 ms* | *+0.4 ms* |

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows:**

| | the set the bump replaces | the published set | the movement |
|---|---|---|---|
| `join-orders`, cold | 97.6 ms | **87.8 ms** (was 100.0 ms) | **−9.8 ms** |
| `join-orders`, resident | 26.6 ms | **24.0 ms** *(was 26.0 ms)* | **−2.6 ms** |
| `sort-topk`, cold | 39.5 ms | **39.2 ms** *(was 39.0 ms)* | **−0.3 ms** |
| `sort-topk`, resident | 2.7 ms | **2.6 ms** *(was 3.1 ms)* | **−0.03 ms** |
| *`q6`, cold — the control* | *15.4 ms* | *16.6 ms* *(was 14.9 ms)* | *+1.7 ms* |
| *`q6-revenue`, cold — the control* | *58.9 ms* | *52.9 ms* *(was 59.1 ms)* | *−6.0 ms* |

**And the incumbents, which this increment did not touch at all:**

| `join-orders`, cold | the set the bump replaces | the published set | this set | the code this increment replaces |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| DuckDB | 91.0 ms | **89.0 ms** *(was 94.0 ms)* | 85.0 ms | 84.0 ms |
| Polars | 219.1 ms | **216.6 ms** (was 225.7 ms) | 197.5 ms | 198.1 ms |
| DataFusion's peer | 168.8 ms | **163.1 ms** (was 175.3 ms) | 144.5 ms | 146.0 ms |

**The code's share is nil, and the incumbents are what say so.** Every difference between this
increment's code and the code it replaces is **inside the spread of the campaigns it is a difference
of**: the join's cold query's **−0.5 ms** against a spread of **2.345 ms** and **2.491 ms**, the sort's
cold **+0.6 ms** against **0.941 ms** and **1.271 ms**, the join's resident **+0.2 ms** against
**0.254 ms** and **0.286 ms**, the sort's resident **0.0 ms** against **0.073 ms** and **0.086 ms**.
**And the incumbents moved by as much**: DuckDB **+1.0 ms**, Polars **−0.6 ms** and the peer
**−1.5 ms** between the two sessions — code this increment cannot have touched. So the band is the
machine's, and the engine's own figures sit inside it.

**And the standing set moved again, and what moved it is the default.** The set the bump
replaces read the join's cold query at **97.6 ms**; the set this file replaces read it at **100.0
ms** — **+2.4 ms** — and the set this file publishes reads it at **87.8 ms**, **−9.8 ms** against the
set the bump replaces and **−12.2 ms** against the set this file replaces. The engine's other rows
moved by less: the join's resident **−2.6 ms**, the sort's cold **−0.3 ms**, the sort's resident
**−0.03 ms**, the `q6` control **+1.2 ms** and the `q6-revenue` control **−6.0 ms**. **And this time
the engine moved more than the incumbents did**: DuckDB **−2.0 ms**, Polars **−2.5 ms** and the
peer **−5.6 ms** — over the same file, on code that neither this increment nor the bump touched.
**And every one of the incumbents' movements is inside the spread the set this file replaces
carried**: DuckDB's **−5.0 ms** against **8.000 ms**, Polars' **−9.1 ms** against **17.946 ms** and the
peer's **−12.1 ms** against **13.506 ms**. **But the engine's own movements are not**: the `q6`
control's **+1.7 ms** is outside the **0.698 ms** that set spread over, and the join's cold
**−12.2 ms** is outside the **7.448 ms** — so it is not the machine's,
and it is not the bump's either: the reader's default was a row limit of **131,072 rows** and it is a
**byte budget of 16 MiB** now, so the batch the reader holds moved with it, and the campaign was
taken again because the batch size touches the figure.

**And what moved the standing set is the machine, the bump and the default.** The set this file
replaces had the noisiest campaigns of the three sets — **7.448 ms** on the join's cold figure, where
the two codes of the A/B spread **2.345 ms** and **2.491 ms** — and the join probe's campaigns of the
A/B session record
a load average of only **2.59–3.47** (`bench-results/join-cost-spec034-run{1,2,3}.json` and
`bench-results/join-cost-spec034-before-run{1,2,3}.json`). The bump's own part is the regenerated
file and the reader it brought, and the default's own part is the batch the reader holds; the
consolidated old-and-new table is in
[§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved).

**So the code's own figures stand, and the standing set's movement is published beside it.** The
re-measurement is published **beside** the published set rather than in place of it, because the
difference between the two is **not** the engine's work: publishing it in its place would have
published a **host's** movement as the engine's. And the standing set's own movement since the bump
is published the same way, for the same reason. The join's stage, taken apart, is in
[§3p](#3p-the-joins-stage-measured-again).
## 3. What the engine's own stages say

The plan output splits the engine's work by stage. The two blocks in [§3b](#3b-the-query-that-no-longer-materialises-anything)
are the **median of three runs of each build**, measured back to back on this machine, so the two are
comparable rather than two runs of the same code on a busy afternoon.

For the Q6 predicate set, resident:

```
stage      rows in  rows out  elapsed
scan       6000000  6000000   0.0 ms
filter     6000000  315203    6.7 ms
project    315203   315203    0.0 ms
aggregate  315203   1         0.0 ms
```

The filter stage does four predicates over 6,000,000 rows. The numeric ones run through the SIMD
kernel at 8.7 G rows/s, which is 0.7 ms each. The two date predicates run through the dictionary
path — one comparison per distinct date, then a map through the indices.

**The kernel is no longer the bottleneck of this query.** That is the point of the project, and it
took two defects to get there: a mask accumulation that extracted two lanes to scalar registers
every two rows (ADR-0006), and a dictionary map that used a mispredicted branch to set a bit. A
single date predicate on its own — the `filter-string` query — now takes 2.4 ms in the filter
stage, against 16.5 ms before the branchless rewrite.


is published the same way, for the same reason. The join's stage, taken apart, is in
[§3p](#3p-the-joins-stage-measured-again).
## 3. What the engine's own stages say

The plan output splits the engine's work by stage. The two blocks in [§3b](#3b-the-query-that-no-longer-materialises-anything)
are the **median of three runs of each build**, measured back to back on this machine, so the two are
comparable rather than two runs of the same code on a busy afternoon.

For the Q6 predicate set, resident:

```
stage      rows in  rows out  elapsed
scan       6000000  6000000   0.0 ms
filter     6000000  315203    6.7 ms
project    315203   315203    0.0 ms
aggregate  315203   1         0.0 ms
```

The filter stage does four predicates over 6,000,000 rows. The numeric ones run through the SIMD
kernel at 8.7 G rows/s, which is 0.7 ms each. The two date predicates run through the dictionary
path — one comparison per distinct date, then a map through the indices.

**The kernel is no longer the bottleneck of this query.** That is the point of the project, and it
took two defects to get there: a mask accumulation that extracted two lanes to scalar registers
every two rows (ADR-0006), and a dictionary map that used a mispredicted branch to set a bit. A
single date predicate on its own — the `filter-string` query — now takes 2.4 ms in the filter
stage, against 16.5 ms before the branchless rewrite.


### 3a. The lazy scan's stages

The same query, the lazy way. There is no filter stage, because the filter ran *inside* the
reader:

```
stage      rows in  rows out  elapsed
scan       6000000  315203    15.4 ms
project    315203   315203    0.0 ms
aggregate  315203   1         0.0 ms
```

The scan stage now carries the file's row count in and the surviving rows out, so the 5.7 million
rows the reader discarded are visible in the plan output rather than implied.

That is the **one-thread** scan. At six workers the same stage takes **4.0 ms**, and the plan
output says `read threads: 6` — the workers that ran, not the threads that were asked for, because
the file's six row groups are the cap. See [§2e](#2e-the-thread-count-sweep).

### 3b. The query that no longer materialises anything

`filter-range` is the query this increment changed, so here are its stages, before and after. **The
projection went from 7.8 ms to 0.0 ms, and the aggregate took over the folding.**

Resident, before:

```
stage      rows in  rows out  elapsed
scan       6000000  6000000   0.0 ms
filter     6000000  2178861   1.5 ms
project    2178861  2178861   7.0 ms
aggregate  2178861  1         2.4 ms
```

Resident, after:

```
stage      rows in  rows out  elapsed
scan       6000000  6000000   0.0 ms
filter     6000000  2178861   1.4 ms
project    2178861  2178861   0.0 ms
aggregate  2178861  1         1.9 ms
```

**The projection's rows in and rows out are still 2,178,861.** The stage did not stop running — it
stopped *copying*. The survivors are still counted, and the aggregate still folds 2,178,861 of them;
it just reads them out of the source instead of out of a column of its own.

And the plan output still names the column:

```
materialised columns: l_quantity
```

It was **read**, so it is named. A column the query read is a column the query read, whether or not
the engine copied it.

The lazy way, the same query:

```
stage      rows in  rows out  elapsed
scan       6000000  2178861   15.1 ms
project    2178861  2178861   0.0 ms
aggregate  2178861  1         2.0 ms
```

There is no filter stage, because the filter ran *inside* the reader — and the projection still
materialises nothing.

**The two builds were measured back to back, on this machine, in the same session.** The CLI's own
median wall clock over five runs:

| | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| resident | 3.3 ms | **3.3 ms** |
| lazy (cold) | 19.9 ms | **17.3 ms** |

The resident row is the **control**: the resident path does not go through the lazy scan, so it did
not move, and the change is the engine's. The lazy figure is close to the number the comparison
harness published for the cold path then — 17.5 ms — and the two are different measurements: the
CLI's is a wall clock over one query in a fresh process, and the harness's measures all the
queries in one process, after a 231 MiB decode has churned the allocator. Both are published; the
harness's is the one the table in §2b uses, because the harness measures the incumbents the same
way.

### 3c. The real Q6's stages, and what the arithmetic costs

The real Q6 has one more stage, and it is the one this increment added:

```
stage      rows in  rows out  elapsed
scan       6000000  407082    67.3 ms
filter     407082   110915    0.3 ms
project    110915   110915    0.9 ms
evaluate   110915   110915    0.2 ms
aggregate  110915   1         0.1 ms
```

**The `evaluate` stage is 0.2 ms of a 70 ms query — 0.3%.** That is the whole cost of the
arithmetic aggregate, and it is small for a reason that was a design decision rather than an
accident: the expression is evaluated over the **110,915 rows that survived the filter**, not
over the 6,000,000 rows of the file. SQL applies `WHERE` before the aggregate, and so does this.

The scan is 67.3 ms because the real Q6 reads `l_extendedprice`, which is the biggest column
in the file. **The cost of the real Q6 is the cost of reading a column, not the cost of
computing with it** — and that is why there is no vectorised arithmetic kernel in this
increment. See [ADR-0012](docs/adr/0012-arithmetic-expression.md).

**The `project` stage is 0.9 ms, and it is not fused away.** The aggregate reads
`l_extendedprice` and `l_discount` through an **expression**, and an expression is evaluated over
the survivors — so its operands have to be aligned with them, and they are gathered. The fusion
in §3b reaches a column that only a **plain** aggregate reads, and not one that an expression
reads.

### 3d. The same query, on six workers

The plan output reports the workers that ran, and the stages show what they did:

```
stage      rows in  rows out  elapsed
scan       6000000  6000000   0.0 ms
filter     6000000  110915    2.1 ms
project    110915   110915    0.9 ms
evaluate   110915   110915    0.3 ms
aggregate  110915   1         0.2 ms
read threads: 1
operator workers: 6 filter, 6 projection
```

**The filter stage falls from 6.3 ms to 2.1 ms — 3.0× from six workers.** The projection falls
from 1.3 ms to 0.9 ms, and the two stages that do not parallelise — `evaluate` and `aggregate` —
are unchanged, because they are not parallelised.

**The `evaluate` and `aggregate` stages do not move, and they are not going to.** `evaluate` is
0.2 ms and `aggregate` is 0.1 ms; together they are 0.3 ms of a 3.1 ms query. Parallelising the
aggregate is forbidden — `SUM` is not associative, and the answer must not depend on the block
boundaries — and parallelising the evaluation would mean slicing the columns into blocks, a copy
per column, to save a fraction of a millisecond.

**The answer is the same bits.** `group [] -> [Float(5526985127.48064)]`, one thread and six, and
the CLI's end-to-end test checks it against DuckDB's answer on the real file.

### 3e. The scan's decomposition, and what the compaction was worth

The plan output reports the scan stage and the pushdown's cost inside it. It does **not** say how
much of the scan is Arrow's own decode and how much is the engine's own work — and that is the
question this increment had to answer before it could fix anything. The probe is in the tree, as
`crates/columnar-engine/examples/filter_range_scan_decomposition.rs`, and it takes the real file,
the real predicates and the real batches:

| | |
|---|---|
| **Arrow's own decode** of `l_quantity` and `l_discount`, with no filter and no conversion | **10.7 ms** |
| **the predicates**, over the whole column | **0.7–1.4 ms** |
| the filter and the copy, in the shape the engine used | **4.7 ms** |
| **the engine's own scan**, end to end | **16.1 ms** — before spec 016, in an earlier campaign; **15.32 ms** now, with the derived batch (§3j) |

**The first three rows are the decomposition and the batch size does not change them** — the same
rows are decoded and the same comparisons are made. The fourth row is end-to-end, and spec 016
moved it: the scan is **15.32 ms** on the same file with the derived batch, against **19.98 ms**
with the fixed one, in the same campaign. That is the fix, and it is in
[§3j](#3j-the-row-group-count-and-what-it-costs).

And the same work in three shapes, on the same file, in the same process:

| | |
|---|---|
| **A — filter the whole batch, then copy** (the shape in the tree) | **4.7 ms** |
| **B — filter only what is kept** | **3.3 ms** |
| **C — compact once, into the chunk** (the shape in the tree now) | **2.5 ms** |

**Two things fall out of that, and they are the whole of this increment.**

**The predicates are not the gap.** 0.7–1.4 ms of a 16 ms scan is 4–9%. The README said the next
thing to do was "narrowing the predicates in the engine's own domain", and that was **wrong** —
there was nothing there to recover. It is corrected in the README rather than quietly dropped.

**Arrow's decode is 10.7 ms and it is the floor.** That is the cost of the bytes, and no
arrangement of the engine's own code goes below it. The engine's own work on top of the decode was
5.4 ms, and that was what was available to win.

And the defect is in the middle of that 5.4 ms: the scan filtered the **whole batch** with Arrow's
`filter_record_batch` and then copied the survivors **a second time** out of the filtered batch. So
it copied the column the query does not materialise — the predicate's column, which was about to be
thrown away — and then copied the survivors again. **Shape C is half of shape A**, and the scan's
stage went from 18.5 ms to 15.1 ms.

**The batch size was measured too, because a constant that depends on the shape of the work has to
be.** The lazy path's batch size has been measured three times now, because the *shape* of the work
has changed three times since the first measurement: the pushdown became the engine's own kernel,
the mask stopped going to the reader as a list of row ranges, and the scan stopped filtering the
whole batch and copying the survivors twice. A constant measured for a shape that no longer exists
is not a measured constant for the shape that does.

**And after spec 016 the batch size is not a constant at all.** The reader's batch size was
**derived from the file** — the largest divisor of the row groups' row count that fits under a
limit — so what was measured was the **limit**, and the batch followed from the file. **The tree has
no row limit now**: the default is **a byte budget** ([§3ag](#3ag-the-width-statement-paid)), the rule
is the width's, and the batch depends on what the read fetches. **The sweep is therefore a ladder of
row counts** — the probe sets each batch itself — and the tree's own batch sits beside it. The probe
sweeps the limit over the two shapes the lazy path serves, prints the batch each limit derives for
the published file, and A/Bs the batch the tree used before against the batch it derives now, with
the order alternating on every round so that a drifting machine cannot bias the result. The raw
reports are `bench-results/batch-size-run{1,2,3}.json` and the tables below are derived from them
by `python3 scripts/medians.py`. The set the file published before is
`bench-results/batch-size-spec050-run{1,2,3}.json`, and the set the code the default replaces was
measured in is `bench-results/batch-size-spec051-before-run{1,2,3}.json`.

**And these campaigns were taken again**, because the reader's default was a row limit of **131,072 rows** and
is a **byte budget of 16 MiB** now, so the batch the reader holds moved with it, and every figure the batch
size touches moved with it — the old code and the new one were measured back to back on this host, three
campaigns each, interleaved. The old-and-new for every family the default moved is in
[§3ah](#3ah-the-default-moved). **And every figure below is published with the figure it moved from**:
the ladder was taken on a different day, and the movement in it is the host's and not the default's —
the probe sets the batch itself, so the default cannot change what a given batch costs.

**One materialised column, one predicate:**

| the limit | the batch it derives | the scan |
|---|---|---|
| 1,024 | 1,024 | 14.45 ms *(was 15.11 ms)* (14.33–14.72) |
| 2,048 | 2,048 | 13.76 ms *(was 14.31 ms)* (13.69–13.89) |
| 4,096 | 4,096 | 14.08 ms *(was 15.01 ms)* (13.93–14.11) |
| 8,192 | 8,000 | 13.53 ms *(was 14.44 ms)* (13.39–13.63) |
| 16,384 | 15,625 | 13.25 ms *(was 14.29 ms)* (12.97–13.27) |
| 32,768 | 31,250 | 12.62 ms *(was 13.63 ms)* (12.49–12.71) |
| 65,536 | 62,500 | 12.46 ms *(was 16.47 ms)* (12.45–12.70) |
| **131,072 — the limit the tree used** | **125,000** | **12.40 ms** *(was 14.89 ms)* (12.29–12.83) |
| 262,144 | 250,000 | 12.44 ms *(was 14.44 ms)* (12.17–12.48) |

**The real Q6's predicate set** — three predicates, one of them on a dictionary-encoded string:

| the limit | the batch it derives | the scan |
|---|---|---|
| 1,024 | 1,024 | 111.62 ms *(was 121.47 ms)* (111.33–116.38) |
| 2,048 | 2,048 | 63.17 ms *(was 67.12 ms)* (61.48–65.51) |
| 4,096 | 4,096 | 38.08 ms *(was 41.26 ms)* (38.02–39.14) |
| 8,192 | 8,000 | 26.44 ms *(was 27.74 ms)* (26.34–26.46) |
| 16,384 | 15,625 | 19.86 ms *(was 20.75 ms)* (19.56–20.05) |
| 32,768 | 31,250 | 16.51 ms *(was 17.44 ms)* (16.42–16.56) |
| 65,536 | 62,500 | 14.90 ms *(was 15.57 ms)* (14.61–15.09) |
| **131,072 — the limit the tree used** | **125,000** | **14.14 ms** *(was 15.88 ms)* (14.11–14.18) |
| 262,144 | 250,000 | 13.74 ms *(was 14.47 ms)* (13.74–13.75) |

**The interleaved A/B is the figure that matters**, because it holds everything else constant and
alternates the order. **Three sets, and every one of them published**, because the A/B is where the
default shows:

| the A/B | one column, one predicate | the real Q6's predicate set |
|---|---|---|
| **this file publishes** — 65,536 against the tree's own **1,000,000** | 12.52 → **12.45 ms**, **−0.6%** | 17.88 → **14.88 ms**, **−16.8%** |
| **the code the default replaces** — 65,536 against its own **125,000** | 12.41 → 12.32 ms, **−0.7%** | 17.72 → 13.92 ms, **−21.5%** |
| **the set the file published before** — 65,536 against **125,000** | 13.38 → 13.95 ms, **+4.3%** | 18.37 → 14.53 ms, **−18.7%** |

**On the published query the tree's own batch is 16.8% faster than the fixed one** — the fixed batch
reads **17.88 ms** where it read **18.37 ms** — and the three campaigns agree to within 0.5 ms on both
sides — 17.79, 17.99 and 17.88 ms against 14.83, 14.88 and 15.31 ms — and the set the bump replaces
had **19.13 ms** against **14.69 ms**, a difference of **−4.43 ms** and **−23.2%**. **On the
one-column shape it is 0.6% faster**, and that shape is the probe's own, not a query this repository
publishes. Why the batch matters at all is in
[§3j](#3j-the-row-group-count-and-what-it-costs): the fixed 65,536-row batch divides no row group's row
count, and the tree's own batch is now a whole row group.

**And the default's own share of the difference between the two codes' own batches.** The **65,536**
rung is the machine's control — the same batch, the same probe, in both campaigns:

| the real Q6's predicate set | the code the default replaces | this file publishes | the difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| 65,536 — the fixed batch, **the control** | 17.72 ms | 17.88 ms | **+0.16 ms** — the machine's |
| the tree's own batch | 13.92 ms, at **125,000** | 14.88 ms, at **1,000,000** | **+0.96 ms** |
| **the code's share** | | | **+0.80 ms** |

**And on the one-column shape the same arithmetic gives +0.02 ms** — the control moved 0.11 ms and the
own batch 0.13 ms — so the default's change is worth nothing on that shape and **0.80 ms on the query
this repository publishes**. **That is the price of the default that moved, and it is named rather
than smoothed over**: the byte budget gives the narrow shapes a whole row group, and on the real Q6's
predicate set a whole row group is dearer than a quarter of one. [§3ah](#3ah-the-default-moved) and
[ADR-0063](docs/adr/0063-the-default-moves-to-the-byte-budget.md) carry it, and the budget's number
is left open — **the byte budget's number, once the reader owns the decode**.

**And the sweep is noisy in this set, which is why the A/B is the figure that matters.** The rows of
the two tables above carry spreads of up to 5.1 ms — the host moved across the campaigns, and the
sweep measures one limit after another — where the A/B alternates the two batches inside the
measurement. **In the set the bump replaces the limit in the tree was the best row on the real Q6's
shape — 14.64 ms against 14.69 ms for 250,000 rows — and within 1% of the best on the one-column
shape, 14.17 ms against 14.10 ms, and the limit 262,144 was rejected for deriving 250,000 rows,
9.9% off that shape's best.** In this set the sweep does separate the limit the tree used from the
best row: the best row on the one-column shape **is** the limit 131,072 at **12.40 ms**, and on the
real Q6's shape it is the limit 262,144 — which derives 250,000 rows — at **13.74 ms**, **2.9%** below
the limit the tree used (**14.14 ms**). **And the tree's own batch is above both**: a whole row group,
**1,000,000 rows**, which the A/B prices at **14.88 ms** — **8.3%** above that shape's best row. **So
the sweep's claim that the limit the tree used was the best of the two is a claim this set does not
make**, and the decision it rested on is not re-opened by that — the rule is a byte budget now, and
its number is the open question above.

### 3f. The scan's read is the Parquet decode, and owning it is not worth it

The cold path's one remaining loss is `filter-range`, by 1.1 ms, and the scan that holds it is 15.7 ms of the 17.5 ms
query. The obvious next move is the one every columnar engine makes eventually: **stop going through Arrow and read
the Parquet file into the engine's own column format.** Arrow's reader is a thin wrapper over the parquet crate's own
reader — it decodes a column chunk into a buffer and *moves* that buffer into an Arrow array — so the engine could use
the parquet crate directly, apply its own mask to the decoded values, and write the survivors straight into its own
column.

It was prototyped as a full read path and measured, and the measurement says **no**. The probe is in the tree, as
`crates/columnar-engine/examples/own_decode_probe.rs`, and it measures four things on the same file, in the same
process, warm, each the median of five runs:

| | |
|---|---|
| **A** | the parquet crate's own decode of the two columns, into plain Rust buffers | **10.30 ms** |
| **B** | Arrow's decode of the same two columns | **9.86 ms** |
| **C** | A, plus the engine's predicate over the decoded buffer, plus the compaction of the survivors into the engine's own column | **15.73 ms** |
| **D** | the engine's own scan, as it is today | **15.74 ms** |

**C is D.** The shape without Arrow is equal to the engine's scan, to a tenth of a millisecond. And **A is B**:
Arrow's reader is not slower than the parquet crate's raw reader, because it *is* the parquet crate's reader with an
array wrapper on top — and the wrapper is worth **0.3 ms** on a 15.7 ms scan.

**So Arrow is not the cost, and the engine keeps Arrow.** The rewrite is refused, and the receipt is published, because
a measurement that says "no" is worth more than the increment it refuses: a future contributor does not have to spend
a week discovering that the Arrow layer is worth 0.3 ms. See
[ADR-0018](docs/adr/0018-the-scan-reads-the-parquet-decode.md).

**The same measurement pointed at where the decode's time is.** The file's columns are **dictionary-encoded** — per
the Parquet footer's own metadata, one row group of 1,000,000 rows:

| column | encodings | uncompressed |
|---|---|---|
| `l_quantity` | PLAIN, RLE, **RLE_DICTIONARY** | 1,667,405 bytes — **1.67 bytes a row** |
| `l_discount` | PLAIN, RLE, **RLE_DICTIONARY** | 503,674 bytes — **0.50 bytes a row** |

A `float64` column at 1.67 bytes a row is not storing floats: it is storing a **dictionary of a few dozen values** and
a million **RLE indices** into it, and the decode is the work of **expanding** those indices back into a million
values.

**And the engine throws most of that expansion away.** The predicate column is expanded into six million floats,
compared row by row, and then dropped — because the query does not materialise it. The engine already knows how to
compare a column without expanding it: the dictionary path compares **one distinct value at a time**, which is why
the predicates cost 1.4 ms rather than 14.

That is the next increment — [spec 012](specs/012-the-scan-does-not-expand-the-predicates-column/spec.md) — and it is
a **hypothesis**, measured before anything is built, the way this one was.

### 3g. The expansion of the RLE indices, and why it is not the decode's cost

The hypothesis §3f ends on was specific: **the predicate's column is expanded into six million values, compared, and
thrown away, and the engine could compare the dictionary instead and apply the mask to the indices.** Spec 012's T3
is the test of it, and the test came out **no**.

The probe is in the tree, as `crates/columnar-engine/examples/rle_expansion_probe.rs`. It measures eleven things on
the same file, in the same process, warm, each the median of five runs, over **three campaigns**; the raw reports are
in `bench-results/rle-expansion-run{1,2,3}.json` and the table below is derived from them by
`python3 scripts/medians.py`. **The set the bump replaces is committed under its own name**,
`bench-results/rle-expansion-spec041-before-run{1,2,3}.json`: the same probe, on `parquet` 54.3.1, on the dataset the
bump replaces.

| | | median (min–max) |
|---|---|---|
| **P** | the parquet crate's own decode of the column | **6.56 ms** (6.43–6.67) |
| **A** | Arrow's decode of the column — what the engine's reader does | **3.34 ms** (3.23–3.53) |
| **PLAIN** | the decode of the **same column written without a dictionary**, through Arrow | **4.37 ms** (4.11–4.41) |
| **V** | the probe's decode: the indices *and* the gather | **6.41 ms** (6.35–6.56) |
| **I** | the probe's decode of the indices **only**: no gather, no values | **4.51 ms** (4.44–4.56) |
| **G** | **the expansion**: the reader's fresh buffers, and the gather | **1.77 ms** (1.73–1.78) |
| **G1** | the reader's fresh buffers, and their zeroing | **0.21 ms** (0.20–0.22) |
| **G2** | the gather alone | **1.58 ms** (1.57–1.60) |
| **M** | **the mask the hypothesis would have to build** from the indices | **1.75 ms** (1.72–1.76) |
| **C** | the hypothesis's shape, end to end | **7.57 ms** (7.53–7.76) |
| **S1** | the engine's scan of the predicate's column | **4.49 ms** (4.47–4.80) |
| | of which the predicate itself | 1.29 ms |
| **S2** | the whole query | **41.14 ms** (40.93–41.62) |

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows** — the same probe, on `parquet` 54.3.1, on the dataset the bump
replaces:

| | | median (min–max) |
|---|---|---|
| **P** | the parquet crate's own decode of the column | 7.20 ms (6.90–7.61) |
| **A** | Arrow's decode of the column — what the engine's reader does | 4.13 ms (3.85–4.29) |
| **PLAIN** | the decode of the **same column written without a dictionary**, through Arrow | 4.69 ms (4.62–4.72) |
| **V** | the probe's decode: the indices *and* the gather | 6.39 ms (6.24–6.52) |
| **I** | the probe's decode of the indices **only**: no gather, no values | 5.09 ms (4.96–5.23) |
| **G** | **the expansion**: the reader's fresh buffers, and the gather | 1.79 ms (1.78–1.81) |
| **G1** | the reader's fresh buffers, and their zeroing | 0.21 ms (0.21–0.22) |
| **G2** | the gather alone | 1.60 ms (1.59–1.62) |
| **M** | **the mask the hypothesis would have to build** from the indices | 1.73 ms (1.72–1.84) |
| **C** | the hypothesis's shape, end to end | 8.02 ms (7.74–8.06) |
| **S1** | the engine's scan of the predicate's column | 5.22 ms (5.10–5.41) |
| | of which the predicate itself | 1.31 ms |
| **S2** | the whole query | 43.84 ms (43.08–44.84) |

**The expansion is real, and it is not the decode's cost.** `l_discount` is a `float64` column stored as **eleven
distinct values** and a million RLE indices into them — four bits a row, 502,051 bytes of indices for a row group of
1,000,000 rows. The expansion is **1.77 ms** of the engine's **3.20 ms** decode of that column, so **55% of it** —
**46%** in the set the bump replaces — and that decode is **7.8% of the query**, which makes the expansion **4.3% of
the 41.14 ms query**.

**The decode's cost is the bytes.** The same column, written without a dictionary, holds 48,018,147 bytes against the
dictionary's 3,012,834, and Arrow decodes it in **4.37 ms** against the dictionary's **3.34 ms**. The dictionary
encoding wins by **1.03 ms** — **0.56 ms** in the set the bump replaces — *despite* paying the 1.77 ms expansion: the
expansion costs less than the forty-five megabytes it saves, which is the whole reason the file is dictionary-encoded.
**The decode with no expansion in it at all is the slower one.**

**The replacement is not cheaper than what it replaces.** The mask is still one bit per row, and the row's bit is the
predicate's answer for the row's index — so the engine has to build it per row, out of the indices. That is **M**:
**1.75 ms**, against the **1.29 ms** the vectorised predicate costs today. There is no cheap "test a bit at a variable
index" instruction, so the mask from the indices is a scalar loop, and it costs **0.46 ms more** than the comparison it
would replace.

**And the indices cannot be had for free.** The parquet crate does not expose them: the expansion is inside
`DictDecoder::get`, its `RowFilter` and `ArrowPredicate` evaluate on the *decoded* batch, `DictIndexDecoder` is in a
private module, and `RleDecoder` is `pub(crate)`. So the engine would have to **own the Parquet reading** — this
spec's own non-goal, and ADR-0018's refusal. The price of owning it is measurable: the probe's own decoder of the
format, checked bit-for-bit against the crate's, is **0.16 ms faster** than the crate's — **0.81 ms** in the set the
bump replaces — and it no longer cancels the increment.

**And the net, in both sets** — the set the bump replaces, and the set this file publishes:

| | the set the bump replaces | the set this file publishes |
|---|---|---|
| the expansion, saved | 1.79 ms | **1.77 ms** |
| the mask from the indices, minus the predicate it replaces | −0.42 ms | **−0.46 ms** |
| **the net, with the crate's own RLE decoder** | **1.37 ms** | **1.31 ms** |
| the probe's RLE decoder, against the crate's | −0.81 ms | **−0.16 ms** |
| **the net, with the decoder the probe actually wrote** | **0.56 ms** | **1.15 ms** |

**So the increment is refused**, and the receipt is published, the way §3f's was: **1.15 ms on a 41.14 ms query**,
where the receipt was **0.56 ms on a 43.84 ms query** in the set the bump replaces, for a rewrite of the read path and
a new implementation of the Parquet format's index encoding. The hypothesis was not wrong about the file — the column
is dictionary-encoded, and the decode does expand six million indices. It was wrong about the size of the prize.

**And the prize grew, and the conclusion does not change.** The expansion's share of the decode moved from **46%** to
**55%**, and the net the rewrite would win from **0.56 ms** to **1.15 ms**: the probe's own decoder is now the faster
one by **0.16 ms** rather than by **0.81 ms**, so the rewrite no longer cancels its own increment. The prize is still
**2.8%** of the query, and the reason the increment is refused is the one above: the mask the rewrite would need costs
**0.46 ms more** than the comparison it would replace, and the expansion it would save is what the dictionary encoding
already buys for the price of forty-five megabytes.

**What moved, and why.** The expansion itself did not move — **1.77 ms** against **1.79 ms** — while the decode around
it did: **3.20 ms** against **3.91 ms**, and Arrow's own decode **3.34 ms** against **4.13 ms**. **Two things moved
together here**: the reader is a different one, 58.4.1 against 54.3.1, and the host was quieter — its load average was
**9.69** in this set's campaigns and **10.86–13.0** in the set the bump replaces. There was no interleaved A/B, so the
two are not separated here; [§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved) says why, and publishes the whole
old-and-new. What this section's own claim rests on is inside one set: the plain column, with no expansion in it at
all, decodes slower than the dictionary's.

One fact an implementation would have to respect, which the probe found the hard way: **every row group has its own
dictionary**, built in the order the values were first seen in that chunk, so two row groups hold the same eleven
values in a different order. The probe's first attempt at the mask was wrong by 3,671 rows because of it.

### 3h. The materialised column, and what it is made of

§3g took the **predicate's** column apart. The query's time is not the predicate's column: on the real Q6 the engine
spends a few milliseconds on the column it filters on and the rest on the column it **materialises** —
`l_extendedprice`, the column the revenue is computed from. Spec 014 takes that column apart with the same
instrument, and then runs the **real Q6** through the engine's own lazy path.

The probe is in the tree, as `crates/columnar-engine/examples/materialised_column_probe.rs`. It measures the same
nine things on three columns of the real Q6, on the same file, in the same process, warm, each the median of five
runs, over **three campaigns**; the raw reports are in `bench-results/materialised-run{1,2,3}.json` and the table
below is derived from them by `python3 scripts/medians.py`. The host's load average was **10.1** during the
campaign — the machine was shared — so the medians are the figures. The set the bump replaces is
`bench-results/materialised-spec041-before-run{1,2,3}.json`.

**The three columns, and how they are written:**

| | `l_discount` | `l_quantity` | `l_extendedprice` |
|---|---|---|---|
| the column's role | predicate | predicate | **materialised** |
| written | `RLE_DICTIONARY` | `RLE_DICTIONARY` | **plain** |
| data pages | 294 dictionary | 294 dictionary | **42 dictionary, 258 plain** |
| dictionary values | 11 | 4,901 | 131,411 |
| index width | 4 bits | 13 bits | 18 bits |
| on disk | 3,023,401 bytes | 9,904,661 bytes | **36,451,424 bytes** |

**The decode, decomposed:**

| | median (min–max) | `l_discount` | `l_quantity` | `l_extendedprice` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **P** | the parquet crate's decode | **6.30 ms** (6.14–6.38) | **6.98 ms** (6.78–7.14) | **36.63 ms** (35.69–37.77) |
| **A** | Arrow's decode | **3.15 ms** (3.11–3.31) | **4.56 ms** (4.55–4.65) | **31.54 ms** *(was 32.84 ms)* |
| **V** | the probe's decode | **6.19 ms** (6.19–6.27) | **8.63 ms** (8.42–8.75) | **36.28 ms** (35.76–36.34) |
| **I** | the probe's indices, no gather | **4.65 ms** (4.26–5.19) | **6.77 ms** (6.58–7.30) | — |
| **G** | the expansion | **1.78 ms** (1.76–1.79) | **1.79 ms** (1.74–1.83) | **1.05 ms** (1.00–1.10) |
| **G1** | the reader's fresh buffers | 0.21 ms (0.20–0.22) | 0.21 ms (0.20–0.22) | 0.23 ms (0.21–0.29) |
| **G2** | the gather, or the copy | **1.55 ms** (1.54–1.56) | **1.52 ms** (1.52–1.61) | **0.79 ms** (0.78–0.90) |
| **X** | the counterfactual, the parquet crate | **22.00 ms** (21.64–22.45) | **35.99 ms** (35.28–36.02) | **39.14 ms** (38.84–39.23) |
| **X** | the counterfactual, Arrow | **19.66 ms** (19.52–19.75) | **33.78 ms** (33.64–34.58) | **34.13 ms** *(was 35.62 ms)* |

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows** — the same probe, on `parquet` 54.3.1, measured
before the dependency moved:

| | median (min–max) | `l_discount` | `l_quantity` | `l_extendedprice` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **P** | the parquet crate's decode | 7.60 ms (7.30–7.87) | 8.46 ms (8.28–9.23) | 36.76 ms (35.22–37.51) |
| **A** | Arrow's decode | 4.62 ms (3.91–4.77) | 6.09 ms (5.57–6.38) | 34.26 ms (33.39–36.47) |
| **V** | the probe's decode | 6.89 ms (6.69–7.14) | 9.24 ms (8.57–9.28) | 36.70 ms (35.67–38.97) |
| **I** | the probe's indices, no gather | 4.61 ms (4.45–5.18) | 7.38 ms (6.68–7.49) | — |
| **G** | the expansion | 1.82 ms (1.77–1.83) | 1.80 ms (1.77–1.90) | 1.11 ms (1.04–1.19) |
| **G1** | the reader's fresh buffers | 0.21 ms (0.20–0.21) | 0.21 ms (0.21–0.22) | 0.23 ms (0.23–0.24) |
| **G2** | the gather, or the copy | 1.55 ms (1.55–1.57) | 1.57 ms (1.56–1.58) | 0.83 ms (0.81–0.83) |
| **X** | the counterfactual, the parquet crate | 23.70 ms (23.67–25.10) | 38.08 ms (35.86–38.83) | 41.19 ms (39.70–43.11) |
| **X** | the counterfactual, Arrow | 22.08 ms (20.11–23.12) | 34.64 ms (33.51–34.70) | 37.03 ms (36.88–38.09) |

**The real Q6, as a ladder.** Every rung is the engine's own scan, through the engine's own lazy path, with the
real Q6's three predicates:

| | median (min–max) | in the set the bump replaces |
|---|---|---|
| **Q0** the predicates alone | **20.16 ms** (19.94–20.28) | 21.31 ms |
| **Q1** the predicates, and the price | **54.09 ms** (53.70–54.94) | 58.00 ms |
| **Q2** the predicates, and the discount | **20.29 ms** (20.07–21.05) | 22.53 ms |
| **Q3** the predicates, and both | **54.48 ms** (53.92–54.83) | 57.58 ms |
| **Q4** the whole query | **52.69 ms** *(was 55.75 ms)* | 59.78 ms |
| of which the scan stage | 53.73 ms | 57.67 ms |
| of which the operator stages | 1.53 ms | 1.77 ms |
| **the materialised column's share** (Q1 − Q0) | **32.37 ms** *(was 34.15 ms)* | 36.69 ms |
| **the aggregate's own share** (Q4 − Q3) | **1.83 ms** | 0.85 ms |

**110,915 rows survive**, and the answer is **5,526,985,127.48064**.

**The materialised column is most of the query, and it is bytes.** 32.37 ms of the 52.69 ms query is the
materialised column, and 60% of the scan stage. Arrow's decode of it is 31.54 ms and the engine's own
materialisation of it is 32.37 ms, so **the engine is 0.83 ms from the floor the Arrow stack sets** — 2.6% above
it. There is nothing in the engine's read path to fix.

**And it is not an expansion of dictionary indices.** The expansion of `l_discount` — the predicate's column — is
**1.78 ms** in the table above, and the materialised column's expansion is **1.05 ms**, of a column whose pages are
mostly plain: the file's writer starts a dictionary for every column and falls back to plain when the dictionary page
reaches its size limit — one megabyte by default. `l_extendedprice` has 131,411 distinct values per row group, so its
dictionary page would be 1.05 MB and the writer abandoned it part-way through the first pages of each row group.

**The counterfactual says that was the right call.** The same column, written with a dictionary and a dictionary
page big enough to keep it:

| | the file's column | the counterfactual | |
|---|---|---|---|
| on disk | **36,451,424 bytes** | 47,499,678 bytes | **30% bigger** |
| Arrow's decode | **31.54 ms** *(was 32.84 ms)* | **34.13 ms** *(was 35.62 ms)* | **8% slower** |

The dictionary is a **loss** for this column. **And the opposite is true of the two predicate columns**: `l_discount`
written plain is 19.66 ms against the dictionary's 3.15 ms — 6.2× slower — and 11,644,415 bytes against
3,023,401; `l_quantity` written plain is 33.78 ms against 4.56 ms, 7.4× slower, and 26,980,304 bytes against
9,904,661. The set the bump replaces had **4.8×** and **5.7×** for the same two comparisons, so the dictionary
paid for itself less well there, and "should this column be dictionary-encoded" is **not a rule, it is a
measurement**: the dictionary pays for a column with a few thousand distinct values and loses for one with a
hundred thousand.

One thing the file does carry for nothing: the writer's fallback leaves the dictionary page in the file —
**6,313,096 bytes** over the six row groups, **2.6%** of the file — that no reader reads. It costs disk and
nothing else, and it is a fact about the *generator*, not about the engine.

**ADR-0018's non-goal stands, and this is the other side of it.** Owning the Parquet reading would buy the
engine the difference between Arrow's decode and the parquet crate's: **3.79 ms** on the materialised column and
**3.15 ms** on `l_discount` — Arrow is already faster than the crate on both. It would also cost the Parquet
format's RLE decoder, which has to match the crate's bit for bit, and the crate does not expose its RLE. The
measurement does not reopen that question: **the materialised column is bytes, and the bytes are what they are.**

**And the instrument had to be fixed before it could measure anything.** At four bits — the predicate's column —
the probe's decoder is competitive with the crate's. At **thirteen bits** it was not: the bit-at-a-time path made
the probe's decode of `l_quantity` **39.55 ms** against the crate's **7.92 ms** — in the set that found the bug —
so the probe was measuring itself. The decoder now reads a group's eight values as one 128-bit word, and the
probe's decode of `l_quantity` is **8.63 ms** against the crate's **6.98 ms**. The probe's decoder is still
verified against the parquet crate's, bit for bit, over every value of every row group, before it measures
anything.

**And the figures moved, and the reason is the bump, the host and the default together.** The query is
**52.69 ms** where it was **55.75 ms** and where the set the bump replaces had **59.78 ms**, and the host's load
average was **10.1** in this set against **12.8** in that one, so the machine's share of the movement is in there too.
The crate's own decode of the materialised column barely moved — **36.76 ms → 36.63 ms** — while Arrow's did, **34.26 ms
→ 31.54 ms**, and the engine's own materialisation of the column closed most of the gap to Arrow's floor: **2.43 ms →
0.83 ms**. **And the reader's default was a row limit of 131,072 rows and is a byte budget of 16 MiB now, so the
campaign was taken again because the batch size touches the figure.** The old-and-new for every family is in
[§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved).

See [ADR-0021](docs/adr/0021-the-materialised-column-and-a-rust-peer.md).

### 3i. The curve over the size of the data

§2e and §2f sweep the **thread count** and §2g sweeps the **load**. This is the third axis: the query and the
thread count are held fixed and the **size of the data** moves. It is the axis a reader asks about when they want
to know what a query costs *per row*, and the table carries that figure beside the timings.

The real Q6 is measured at **three dataset sizes** — 6, 12 and 24 million rows — generated by this repository's
own generator from the pinned seed 24001, with the scale factor, the row count and the byte count recorded in each
manifest. The generator is **byte-deterministic**: regenerating scale factor one at seed 24001 writes
`data/lineitem.parquet` and `data/orders.parquet` byte for byte, with the same SHA-256 they carry now. Every
system is measured at every size, in the same harness, on the same file, with the thread count pinned to one. The
raw reports are in `bench-results/curve-run{1,2,3}.json` and the table below is derived from them by
`python3 scripts/medians.py`.

| rows | sf | MiB | **engine** | engine scan | engine ops | duckdb | polars | datafusion | **ms/Mrow** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 6,000,000 | 1 | 231.6 | **53.1 ms** *(was 47.9 ms)* | 47.2 ms | 1.4 ms | 61.0 ms | 183.4 ms | 100.8 ms | **8.9** |
| 12,000,000 | 2 | 465.4 | **106.3 ms** *(was 97.5 ms)* | 94.3 ms | 2.7 ms | 121.0 ms | 358.3 ms | 199.0 ms | **8.9** |
| 24,000,000 | 4 | 936.2 | **211.7 ms** *(was 193.6 ms)* | 187.6 ms | 5.6 ms | **265.0 ms** *(was 244.0 ms)* | 716.9 ms | 396.9 ms | **8.8** |

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows** — the same code, on `parquet` 54.3.1, on the datasets the
bump replaces:

| rows | sf | MiB | **engine** | engine scan | engine ops | duckdb | polars | datafusion | **ms/Mrow** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 6,000,000 | 1 | 231.6 | **57.7 ms** | 56.8 ms | 1.6 ms | 69.0 ms | 197.4 ms | 114.6 ms | **9.6** |
| 12,000,000 | 2 | 465.4 | **114.3 ms** | 111.5 ms | 2.9 ms | 139.0 ms | 390.1 ms | 223.5 ms | **9.5** |
| 24,000,000 | 4 | 936.2 | **232.2 ms** | 226.3 ms | 5.9 ms | 268.0 ms | 774.7 ms | 451.1 ms | **9.7** |

The stage columns are the last run of each campaign and the total is the median over five runs, so at the smallest
size the two disagree by 1% — that is the run-to-run spread on a shared machine, not a disagreement about the
work.

**The cost per row is flat, and that is the finding.** 8.9, 8.9 and 8.8 ms a million rows: the engine's cost
does **not** amortise as the file grows. It is exactly what §3h predicts — the query's cost is **bytes**, and
bytes cost what they cost — and it is the answer to the question a reader asking about the size axis has. A curve
that fell would have said the fixed costs dominate; this one does not fall.

**The engine is ahead of every incumbent at every size.** DuckDB by 1.15×, 1.14× and 1.25×; Polars by 3.45×, 3.37×
and 3.39×; and the Rust peer by 1.90×, 1.87× and 1.87×. The margin over DuckDB is **about the same at every size**
— 1.14× to 1.25× — where the set the bump replaces has 1.15× to 1.22× and DuckDB relatively better on the bigger
files. In this campaign it is not: the engine's margin over DuckDB is 1.15× at six million rows and 1.25× at
twenty-four million.

**The scan is the query at every size**, and the operators grow from 1.4 ms to 5.6 ms as the rows that survive the
filter grow — and they stay at **2–3%** of it. The arithmetic aggregate is not the bottleneck at any size, which is
what §3c found at one size and this confirms at three.

**These figures moved, and the reason is the host, the bump and the default together.** The reader's default was a
row limit of **131,072 rows** and is a **byte budget of 16 MiB** now, so the campaign was taken again because the
batch size touches the figure. The engine's six-million-row figure is
**53.1 ms**, where it was **47.9 ms** and where the set the bump replaces has **57.7 ms**; DuckDB's
is **61.0 ms** against **69.0 ms**, Polars'
**183.4 ms** against **197.4 ms**, and the peer's **100.8 ms** against **114.6 ms**. **The incumbents do not read
the file through this repository's Arrow**, so their movement is the **host's** — and the host was quieter here: this
campaign ran at a load average of **2.15 to 2.44**, where the campaigns that published the rest of this file ran at
**10.1** and above. The engine's read is `parquet`'s reader, so its own figure is the one the bump touches
directly. **The comparison is what holds**, and it holds in both sets: the engine is ahead of every incumbent at
every size.

**And the same query on the same file is 52.69 ms in [§3h](#3h-the-materialised-column-and-what-it-is-made-of)'s
ladder**, where it was **55.75 ms**, measured in a campaign that ran at a load average of **10.1**. Two numbers for
one measurement, and the difference is the host's — which is why the curve's own campaign carries the incumbents,
and why the load average is in the report.

**One thing the curve found that is not a timing: the engine's default memory budget refuses the real Q6 at 24
million rows.** The engine estimates the working set **before it reads a row** and refuses the query when the
estimate does not fit: at 24 million rows the estimate is **392.0 MiB** — where it was **377.7 MiB** — against the
**256 MiB** default, so the query is rejected having read nothing at all, and at 12 million rows the estimate is
**203.2 MiB**, where it was **188.8 MiB**, which fits.
The estimate is the engine's own and every point of the curve carries it; the budget the curve was measured under
is **1024 MiB**, so that the largest size runs at all. The budget is a **policy knob**, not a limit on the engine:
below it, it changes nothing at all.

**And this section used to publish 391 MiB there, which was wrong.** The figure was **derived** — *about 16 bytes
a row*, times the row count — and the multiplication was **13.3 MiB** out of the estimate this section published
then, **377.7 MiB**, which was **16.5 bytes a row**. **The estimate has moved since — 392.0 MiB, 17.1 bytes a row —
and it moved with the default**, which now charges the estimate for the batch the read will hold; the bump
changed the file's metadata and not its rows or its columns, so **the bump did not move it** — it was wrong
before the bump too. The correction is recorded in
[ADR-0052](docs/adr/0052-the-estimate-that-was-derived-instead-of-measured.md).

### 3j. The row-group count, and what it costs

§2e says the engine's parallelism is capped by the **file's** row-group count, and until this
increment that sentence was an inference from a single file with six row groups. The row-group
size is now a **knob**: `columnar-cli dataset --row-group-rows N` writes the file with N rows per
row group, the manifest records both N and the number of row groups written, and the benchmark
report carries the row-group count so that a sweep says how many row groups the file it swept
had. The default is unchanged — a million rows, six row groups — so every figure published before
this increment is still reproducible by the command that made it.

Three files were generated from the same seed, the same six million rows and the same schema, and
they differ in one thing: **six, twelve and twenty-four row groups**. All three were swept and
probed in **one session**, interleaved, so that a difference between them is a difference between
the files rather than a difference between two times on a shared machine.

**Every figure in this section is this set's**, measured after the dependency bump and on the
regenerated file; where a figure moved, the value it replaces is named beside it, and the whole
old-and-new is collected in [§3x](#3x-the-bump-and-the-figures-it-moved).

**The cap is `min(threads, row_groups)`, and it is now measured rather than inferred:**

| threads | 6 groups: workers | 6 groups: engine | 12 groups: workers | 12 groups: engine | 24 groups: workers | 24 groups: engine |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | **13.2 ms** *(was 14.7 ms)* | 1 | **13.0 ms** *(was 14.4 ms)* | 1 | **13.7 ms** *(was 14.6 ms)* |
| 2 | 2 | 6.7 ms *(was 7.5 ms)* | 2 | 7.0 ms *(was 7.9 ms)* | 2 | 7.1 ms *(was 8.5 ms)* |
| 4 | 4 | 4.9 ms *(was 5.4 ms)* | 4 | 4.0 ms *(was 4.7 ms)* | 4 | 4.2 ms *(was 5.1 ms)* |
| 6 | 6 | 3.9 ms *(was 4.1 ms)* | 6 | 3.9 ms *(was 4.2 ms)* | 6 | 3.9 ms *(was 4.5 ms)* |
| 8 | **6** | 3.9 ms *(was 4.3 ms)* | **8** | 3.7 ms *(was 4.0 ms)* | **8** | 4.5 ms |
| 14 | **6** | 3.9 ms *(was 4.0 ms)* | **12** | **5.0 ms** *(was 5.1 ms)* | **14** | 5.3 ms |

The workers column is `min(threads, row_groups)` at every point of every sweep, and nothing else
explains it: the same file, the same query, the same machine, the same harness, and the worker
count follows the row-group count exactly.

**And the file's row-group count no longer costs the engine anything.** The engine's one-thread
figure across the three files is **13.2, 13.0 and 13.7 ms** — within 6% of each other, where the
set the bump replaces had **15.4, 15.7 and 16.4 ms**. The ladder below is the same measurement with
the batch the tree derives now — **the whole row group**, where it was **125,000** rows — and it is where the
row groups cost what they cost.

**The same thing, measured back to back.** The per-row-group cost probe puts the engine's scan at
two batch sizes in one session: the fixed 65,536 rows the tree used, and the batch the tree derives
now. Same build, same file, same process:

| the engine's scan, one thread | 6 groups | 12 groups | 24 groups |
|---|---|---|---|
| the fixed 65,536 rows — the defect | **17.66 ms** *(was 18.30 ms)* | **21.86 ms** *(was 22.42 ms)* | **29.73 ms** *(was 30.62 ms)* |
| **the derived batch — the fix** | **14.20 ms** *(was 14.73 ms)* | **13.64 ms** *(was 14.65 ms)* | **14.13 ms** *(was 15.18 ms)* |

**The defect's cost is 3.5, 8.2 and 15.6 ms**, where the set the bump replaces had 4.7, 9.5 and
18.5 ms, and the fix's cost is **flat within 4.1%** — 14.20, 13.64 and 14.13 ms, where the 125,000-row
batch read 14.73, 14.65 and 15.18 ms, against the set the bump replaces' 15.32, 15.13 and 15.51 ms. That
is the whole of the finding, in one table: the
same measurement, the same session, the same machine, one row of it broken and one row of it
fixed. The raw reports are `bench-results/row-group-cost-{6,12,24}-run{1,2,3}.json`, and
`python3 scripts/medians.py` derives the table from them.

**And the ladder says what the mechanism is.** The same probe runs the engine's own scan at eight
reader batch sizes, on each of the three files:

| the reader's batch | 6 groups | 12 groups | 24 groups | ms a row group |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 12,500 | 22.32 ms | 22.93 ms | 23.19 ms | 0.048 |
| 25,000 | 17.60 ms | 18.26 ms | 18.83 ms | 0.069 |
| 31,250 | 17.04 ms | 18.01 ms | 18.44 ms | 0.078 |
| 50,000 | 15.52 ms | 16.11 ms | 17.13 ms | 0.089 |
| 62,500 | 15.12 ms | 15.49 ms | 16.06 ms | 0.053 |
| **65,536** — divides none of them | **17.66 ms** *(was 18.30 ms)* | **21.86 ms** *(was 22.42 ms)* | **29.73 ms** *(was 30.62 ms)* | **0.671** |
| **the whole row group — the fix** | **14.20 ms** *(was 14.73 ms)* | **13.64 ms** *(was 14.65 ms)* | **14.13 ms** *(was 15.18 ms)* | **0.025** |
| 250,000 | 14.11 ms | 14.49 ms | **14.13 ms** *(was 14.73 ms)* | 0.034 |

**Seven of the eight batch sizes divide the row group's row count, and every one of them costs
0.02–0.09 ms a row group**, where the set the bump replaces had 0.01–0.07. **The eighth — 65,536,
which divides none of 1,000,000, 500,000 or 250,000 — costs 0.67**, where it cost **0.78**. The
mechanism is a property of the *shape* of the number and not of its size: a batch size that
divides the row group's row count ends the row group on a batch boundary, and one that does not
leaves a **partial** batch at the end of every row group.

**And the attribution says which column pays it.** The crate's read of each column, on its own, at
the fixed batch — and the engine's own scan, at the derived batch, beside it:

| | 6 groups | 12 groups | 24 groups | ms a row group |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the crate's read of `l_shipdate` | **9.24 ms** *(was 9.71 ms)* | 13.29 ms | **20.80 ms** *(was 21.17 ms)* | **0.642** *(was 0.637)* |
| the crate's read of `l_discount` | 3.39 ms | 3.26 ms | 3.24 ms | −0.009 |
| the crate's read of `l_extendedprice` | 3.04 ms | 2.99 ms | 3.07 ms | 0.002 |
| **the engine's own scan, the derived batch** | **14.11 ms** *(was 14.22 ms)* | **13.64 ms** *(was 14.65 ms)* | **14.08 ms** *(was 15.00 ms)* | **−0.002** |

**`l_shipdate` alone carries it.** The other two columns' reads are flat across the three files —
−0.009 and 0.002 ms a row group — and the engine's own scan, at the derived batch, is flat at
**−0.002 ms** a row group. Which of `l_shipdate`'s properties makes it the one that pays — its string
dictionary, its page count, or its size — **is not separated by this measurement**, and it is
written down as the next question rather than guessed at.

**The incumbents are the control.** Their cold scans did not pay the defect, and their figures are
the host's rather than the engine's:

| at one thread | 6 groups | 12 groups | 24 groups |
|---|---|---|---|
| **the engine** | **13.2 ms** *(was 14.7 ms)* | **13.0 ms** *(was 14.4 ms)* | **13.7 ms** *(was 14.6 ms)* |
| duckdb | **29.0 ms** *(was 31.0 ms)* | **27.0 ms** *(was 29.0 ms)* | **27.0 ms** *(was 29.0 ms)* |
| polars | **179.2 ms** *(was 191.8 ms)* | **177.0 ms** *(was 189.0 ms)* | **178.6 ms** *(was 186.2 ms)* |
| datafusion | **32.2 ms** *(was 35.7 ms)* | **33.4 ms** *(was 37.8 ms)* | **35.1 ms** *(was 37.5 ms)* |

`DuckDB`'s and `Polars`' figures move the other way — down — and the peer's is the one that rises
with the row-group count, 0.1 ms a row group, where the engine's own read does not rise at all:
**13.2 ms against 13.7 ms**. This measurement does not separate the peer's share from the host's. What it does show is
that the engine's own read does not pay for the row groups it reads: the rows, the seed and the
schema are the same in all three files.

**What the fix was.** The parquet crate's reader reads a row group at a time, and the reader's
**batch size** decides where a batch ends. When the batch size does not divide a row group's row
count, the row group's last batch is **partial** — and **a partial batch costs 0.68 ms** on this
shape, where the set the bump replaces had **0.78 ms**. The engine's batch size was 65,536 rows,
which divides none of 1,000,000, 500,000 and 250,000, so the engine paid that once per row group.
The reader's batch size is now **the largest divisor of the row groups' row count that fits under
the limit**, so no row group ends with a partial batch. The full account is in
[ADR-0023](docs/adr/0023-the-per-row-group-cost.md).

**The encoding changed too, and it is a small effect.** The writer's one-megabyte dictionary page
limit is **per row group**, so a smaller row group carries a smaller dictionary, and the
high-cardinality column's dictionary fits where it did not before. The same probe that took the
materialised column apart in §3h, run on all three files:

| `l_extendedprice` | 6 groups | 12 groups | 24 groups |
|---|---|---|---|
| written | **plain** | **plain** | **plain** |
| pages | 42 dictionary, 258 plain | 84 dictionary, 216 plain | 168 dictionary, 144 plain |
| dictionary-page bytes — **read by nobody** | 6,313,096 | 12,627,464 | **25,259,152** |
| plain-data bytes | 41,646,592 | 35,290,624 | 22,578,688 |
| Arrow's decode | **31.69 ms** *(was 33.46 ms)* | 33.76 ms | **33.06 ms** *(was 35.62 ms)* |
| **the materialised column's share of the query** | **34.78 ms** | **34.85 ms** | **35.94 ms** |

The smaller row groups really do make the materialised column *more* dictionary-encoded — 168
dictionary pages against 42 — and they really do leave **four times as many dead dictionary pages**
in the file, 25.3 MB that no reader reads. But the column's decode moves by **4%** and **its share
of the query by 3%** — 34.78 ms against 35.94 ms, where the set the bump replaces had the share
flat — so the encoding is a small effect in this set. The predicates' cost moves by **6.9%** — 18.46
ms against 19.74 ms, where it was 19.99 ms against 21.23 ms — and what is left of *that* is the
dictionary work per row group, not the partial batch.

**And the query itself, through the engine's own lazy path, as the ladder §3h uses:**

| the real Q6 | 6 groups | 12 groups | 24 groups |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Q0** the predicates alone | **18.46 ms** *(was 19.99 ms)* | **18.68 ms** *(was 20.47 ms)* | **19.74 ms** *(was 21.23 ms)* |
| **Q4** the whole query | **52.68 ms** *(was 56.10 ms)* | **53.40 ms** *(was 56.02 ms)* | **55.65 ms** *(was 58.46 ms)* |
| of which the scan stage | 54.57 ms | 54.78 ms | 56.22 ms |

**What is left is not the partial batch, and this measurement says so.** The two-predicate shape —
the one the sweep and the cost probe run — is flat across the three files: **14.7, 14.4 and 14.6
ms**, and **14.20, 13.64 and 14.13 ms** in the probe's own back-to-back ladder, where the 125,000-row
batch read **14.73, 14.65 and 15.18 ms**. The real Q6's
**three**-predicate shape still rises by about **0.07 ms a row group**, and the two shapes differ by
exactly one thing: the third predicate's column, `l_quantity`, which is dictionary-encoded with
4,901 values a row group. So what remains is **dictionary work per row group** — the crate
building that dictionary, and the engine comparing its distinct values — and not the partial batch.

**Which of the two it is is not separated by this measurement**, and it is written down as the next
spec's question rather than guessed at: the crate's per-row-group dictionary build, or the
engine's per-row-group comparison of the dictionary's distinct values. The incumbents' three-predicate
figures on these files were not measured, so this file does not claim that they do not pay it.

**The knob works, the published claim is confirmed, and the price is now the encoding and the
dictionary.** The row-group count is a knob; the parallelism above six workers was real and is
measured; the engine's read no longer pays for the row groups it reads; and the remaining per-row-group
cost is 0.07 ms of dictionary work, on a query that reads three predicate columns.

**The engine's speedup at fourteen threads is 3.40×, 2.57× and 2.60×** — and it does not *rise*
with the row-group count, which is the point: it was **3.74×, 3.63× and 3.39×** in the set the
bump replaces, and it used to rise, because the engine's **serial** cost grew with the row groups:
more row groups meant more of the defect, and a bigger serial part means a bigger ratio. Now the
serial part is flat, so the speedup is set by the parallelism alone — and the cap above it is still
`min(threads, row_groups)`.

**The per-row-group cost is gone.** On the probe's own back-to-back ladder it is **0.67 ms** with
the fixed batch and **0.02 ms** with the derived one — a 96% reduction. Spec 015 published the same
defect on this sweep's one-thread Q6 across the three files; the sweep's one-thread figures are now
**13.2, 13.0 and 13.7 ms**, within 6% of each other, so there is no per-row-group cost left to
measure there either.

**The best figure is the six-row-group file's again.** It was **4.1 ms** there in the set the bump
replaces, against **4.3 ms** on the twelve-group file and **4.8 ms** on the twenty-four-group one,
and it is **4.0 ms** there now, against **5.1 ms** and **5.3 ms**. The absolute figures are this
set's, but the *one-thread* figures, which are the ones the defect was in, are flat: **14.6, 14.4
and 15.9 ms**.

**These figures moved, and the reason is the bump, the host and the default together.** The row-group probe
reports the host's own load average: **7.61–8.71** while these campaigns ran, against
**9.83–13.17** for the set the bump replaces, so the machine was quieter for them. The engine's
read is `parquet`'s reader, and 58.4.1 is a different reader from 54.3.1, so the engine's figures
are the ones the bump touches directly; the incumbents are the yardstick, and they moved with the
host rather than with the reader — `DuckDB`'s cold scan on the six-group file fell,
at **31.0 ms**, where it read **33.0 ms**, `Polars`' moved from **193.6 ms** to **191.8 ms**, where it
read **197.2 ms** in the set the file published before, and the peer's from **37.4 ms** to **35.7 ms**. **And the
reader's default was a row limit of 131,072 rows and is a byte budget of 16 MiB now, so the campaign was taken again
because the batch size touches the figure.** The comparison is what holds, and it holds: the engine's one-thread
figure is flat across the three files, and the defect is gone.

**That last part was a defect, and spec 016 fixed it.** A defect gets its own spec: the engine's
read path had a per-row-group cost that the Parquet readers the incumbents use do not have, and
spec 016 measured the mechanism and removed it. See
[ADR-0023](docs/adr/0023-the-per-row-group-cost.md).

### 3k. The dictionary, counted

§3j left one cost unattributed. The real Q6's **three**-predicate shape rises by about **0.07 ms a
row group** across the three files while the **two**-predicate shape is flat, and the two shapes
differ by exactly one thing: `l_quantity`, the third predicate's column, which the Parquet writer
fell back to dictionary-encoding. Two things could be paying that cost, and they have opposite
answers — the **crate** rebuilding the column's dictionary per row group, or the **engine**
comparing the dictionary's distinct values per row group. The first is a dependency and cannot be
fixed here; the second is the engine's own.

This increment separates them with an instrument rather than an argument.
`crates/columnar-engine/examples/dictionary_cost_probe.rs` reads the same file in the same process
and times the crate's decode and the engine's comparison separately, in **two shapes of the same
read**: the **expanded** one, where the reader expands the dictionary-encoded columns into plain
values and the engine compares the values, and the **kept** one, where the reader keeps the columns
dictionary-encoded and the engine compares the **distinct values** and maps the answer back through
the indices. The kept shape is the only way the engine could stop comparing every row, so it is
measured rather than argued about.

The probe reads at the **batch size the engine itself would use for the file** — asked of the
engine rather than copied from a rule — so its shapes are the engine's shapes. **And the rule is a
byte budget now**, so that batch is **500,000** rows on the six- and twelve-row-group files and
**250,000** on the twenty-four-row-group one, where the old rule's row limit gave **125,000** rows
everywhere. And it checks that the two paths answer the same question before it times either:
**both paths keep 110,915 rows**.

**The reading, and the engine's comparison on top of it** — the Q6 revenue query's three predicates,
on the six-, twelve- and twenty-four-row-group files (`data/lineitem.parquet`,
`data/lineitem-rg12.parquet`, `data/lineitem-rg24.parquet`), the medians of three campaigns each:

| the shape | 6 groups | 12 groups | 24 groups |
|---|---|---|---|
| the crate's read, with the dictionaries expanded | **13.81 ms** | **14.24 ms** | **14.66 ms** |
| the crate's read, with the dictionaries **kept** | **200.19 ms** | **198.71 ms** | **199.64 ms** |
| the crate's read, and the engine's comparison over the values | **18.63 ms** | **18.51 ms** | **19.62 ms** |
| the crate's read, and the engine's comparison over the **distinct values** | **206.41 ms** | **204.45 ms** | **203.16 ms** |
| **the engine's own read** | **18.40 ms** | **18.71 ms** | **19.62 ms** |

**The set this file published before, for the same rows,** held **14.30 ms**, **15.64 ms** and
**15.72 ms** for the crate's read with the dictionaries expanded, **210.85 ms**, **206.02 ms** and
**212.48 ms** for the crate's read with the dictionaries kept, **19.94 ms**, **19.99 ms** and
**21.21 ms** with the engine's comparison over the values, **224.00 ms**, **213.16 ms** and
**217.55 ms** over the distinct values, and **19.56 ms**, **20.90 ms** and **21.33 ms** for the
engine's own read. **And the set the code the default replaces was measured in** — the same probe,
the old rule's batch, on this host, interleaved with the campaigns above — holds **13.56 ms**,
**14.00 ms** and **14.91 ms**, **195.38 ms**, **192.43 ms** and **196.14 ms**, **19.02 ms**,
**19.30 ms** and **20.19 ms**, **206.32 ms**, **198.80 ms** and **209.89 ms**, and **18.93 ms**,
**19.42 ms** and **20.37 ms**.

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same rows** — the same probe, on `parquet` 54.3.1, measured
before the dependency moved:

| the shape | 6 groups | 12 groups | 24 groups |
|---|---|---|---|
| the crate's read, with the dictionaries expanded | 15.46 ms | 15.87 ms | 16.65 ms |
| the crate's read, with the dictionaries **kept** | 282.25 ms | 285.01 ms | 287.73 ms |
| the crate's read, and the engine's comparison over the values | 21.40 ms | 21.34 ms | 22.36 ms |
| the crate's read, and the engine's comparison over the **distinct values** | 298.88 ms | 286.81 ms | 291.92 ms |
| **the engine's own read** | 21.40 ms | 21.25 ms | 22.62 ms |

**And the alternative is not a little slower: it is 14.5×.** On the six-row-group file the kept
read is 200.19 ms, where it was 210.85 ms, against the expanded read's 13.81 ms, where it was
14.30 ms, and with the engine's comparison on top of each the whole path is 206.41 ms, where it
was 224.00 ms, against 18.63 ms, where it was 19.94 ms — **11.1×**. The set the bump replaces had
**18.3×** and **14.0×** for the same two comparisons.

**The kept read is where the movement is.** The crate's kept read of the six-row-group file is
**200.19 ms**, where it was **210.85 ms**, and the set the bump replaces had **282.25 ms** — a
quarter less — while the expanded read moved from 15.46 ms to **13.81 ms**, where it was
**14.30 ms**. The host was no quieter for the kept read's movement out of the replaced set: the
load average was **9.47–8.25** in the set this file published before and **3.08–3.00** in this one,
against **7.17–7.70** in the replaced one, so that movement is not the machine's.

**And the code's share of it is the old code's own campaigns.** Measured back to back with the new
one, on this host, the old rule's batch reads the expanded shape at **13.56 ms** where the new
rule's reads it at **13.81 ms**, and the kept shape at **195.38 ms** where the new one reads
**200.19 ms** — so the batch the default moved is worth **0.25 ms** on the expanded read and
**4.81 ms** on the kept one, and the rest of the movement is the host's.

**The mechanism is in the reader, and it is measured.** The crate hands back a **new** dictionary
array for every batch: **0 of 11** consecutive batches share the same array on the six- and
twelve-row-group files, **0 of 23** on the twenty-four-row-group one, on all three files and in
both versions of the code. The probe checks this on `l_shipdate`, the one column the file keeps
as a dictionary, and it checks it by pointer identity rather than by comparing the values. So
"compare the distinct values once a row group" is impossible with this reader: the engine cannot
know the dictionary has not changed, because the reader gives it a different one every time. **The
alternative is closed, and the expanded shape is kept.**

**Per column, the same story and one exception.** The six-row-group file, one column at a time:

| column | expanded | kept | |
|---|---|---|---|
| `l_quantity` | **4.19 ms** *(was 4.56 ms)* | **110.29 ms** *(was 115.37 ms)* | **26.3× slower** |
| `l_discount` | **3.10 ms** *(was 3.20 ms)* | **82.34 ms** *(was 84.91 ms)* | **26.6× slower** |
| `l_shipdate` | **6.17 ms** *(was 6.55 ms)* | **6.09 ms** *(was 6.48 ms)* | **the same** — the file's schema already says dictionary |

**And the set the bump replaces, for the same columns:**

| column | expanded | kept | |
|---|---|---|---|
| `l_quantity` | 5.09 ms | 177.47 ms | 34.9× slower |
| `l_discount` | 3.75 ms | 99.35 ms | 26.5× slower |
| `l_shipdate` | 6.38 ms | 6.32 ms | the same — the file's schema already says dictionary |

`l_shipdate` is the one column the generator wrote as a dictionary, so the kept reader hands it
over as a dictionary either way, and keeping it costs nothing. That is the one case where the
dictionary is free, and it is the exception that proves the rule: **the price of the kept shape is
the crate building a dictionary it was not asked to keep.**

**And the engine's own work over the dictionaries is not what makes the kept shape slow.** The
probe times the two halves of the engine's dictionary path on their own, over the whole file:

| | 6 groups | 12 groups | 24 groups |
|---|---|---|---|
| the distinct values, compared | **0.32 ms** *(was 1.31 ms)* | **0.32 ms** *(was 1.28 ms)* | **0.62 ms** *(was 1.29 ms)* |
| the map, from the answer to the rows | **7.12 ms** *(was 7.14 ms)* | **7.30 ms** *(was 7.06 ms)* | **6.87 ms** *(was 7.13 ms)* |

The set the bump replaces had **1.43, 1.33 and 1.37 ms** for the distinct values and **7.27,
7.16 and 7.19 ms** for the map. The kept shape's cost is in the crate's read — **200.19 ms** of
