See [§3c](#3c-the-real-q6s-stages-and-what-the-arithmetic-costs) and [ADR-0012](docs/adr/0012-arithmetic-expression.md). |
| **Window functions, decimal, dates, nested types, GPU** | Out of scope for spec 001. | The **hash join and the sort** were in this list and are built now — spec 020 — and the two queries that use them are published in [§2](#2-the-engine-against-duckdb-polars-and-datafusion) with the rest. The others are listed in the spec's out-of-scope table. |

---

## 5a. The performance gate

The constitution requires two gates — no statistically significant regression against a stored
baseline, and absolute budgets met — and until spec 004 neither existed. They do now, and they
are worth describing because of what they found.

`just perf-gate` measures the kernel, 32 independent blocks of 5 timed passes each, and compares
the samples against `bench-results/kernel-baseline.json` with a **Mann-Whitney U test**: the
exact permutation distribution for small samples, the normal approximation with tie and
continuity corrections for large ones. It is one-sided, in the direction "the current sample is
slower".

**The first version was statistically correct and useless.** Five consecutive runs against an
unchanged baseline produced:

| flavor | runs reporting a regression | the difference |
|---|---|---|
| `cpp-auto` | 5 of 5 | 1.3% – 2.3% slower |
| `cpp-simd` | 4 of 5 | 1.7% – 3.9% slower |
| `scalar` | 4 of 5 | 0.9% – 1.2% slower |
| `rust-simd` | 0 of 5 | — |

**Thirteen false positives in twenty checks, on code that had not changed.** The test was not
wrong: the machine's throughput drifts by a percent or two between one moment and the next, and
a test with 32 samples on each side detects that drift as significant every time.

So a regression now has to be **both** statistically significant **and** practically significant,
and the practical threshold is set from that measurement: **5%**, above the observed drift of
0.9%–3.9%. With it, five consecutive runs on unchanged code produce **zero** failures, and the
differences that remain are reported as *within the noise* rather than hidden.

The gate has four answers — no regression, within the noise, regression, and **inconclusive** —
and the last one is not a pass. It says the gate could not tell, which is more useful than a pass
that was decided by a threshold nobody examined.

**And the gate was green while this campaign ran.** It measured `cpp-simd` at **8.628 G rows/s**
against a stored baseline of **8.306 G**, and `scalar` at **3.532 G** against **3.469 G** — above
the baseline in both cases, where the set this campaign replaced ran with every flavour **46–53%
slower** than that same baseline. The baselines are the medians of the stored samples in
`bench-results/kernel-baseline.json`, and the gate's own readings for this campaign are in
[The conditions](#the-conditions). The gate is what says the machine was not thrashing while this
file's figures were taken, and it is why they are about 40% faster than the set they replace.

## 6. Reproducing this

```bash
just dataset       # 6,000,000 rows, deterministic, about 2.8 seconds
just bench         # the kernel A/B -> bench-results/kernel.json
just compare       # DuckDB and Polars  -> bench-results/compare.json

just check-x86     # compile the x86-64 half of the kernel, from this ARM64 Mac
just verify-avx2   # run the AVX2 path under Rosetta 2 and check it against the oracle

python3 scripts/medians.py   # the published table, derived from the committed reports

just perf-gate      # the performance gate: measure, and compare against the baseline
just perf-baseline  # rewrite the baseline from a fresh measurement
```

`just compare` needs `DuckDB` on `PATH` or in `tools/bin/`, and the `polars` Python package
installed. Both versions are recorded in the report.

**A recipe's arguments are positional**, in the order the recipe declares them: `just` assigns the
first argument to the first parameter, and it does not read `name=value` as an assignment. A recipe
that takes a file and an output takes the file first.

**The row-group files of §3j are made by the same generator with one flag different:**

```bash
just dataset                       # the published file: six row groups
just dataset-row-groups 500000     # twelve row groups
just dataset-row-groups 250000     # twenty-four row groups

just sweep "data/lineitem-rg250k.parquet" "q6" "1,2,4,6,8,14" \
    "3" "cold" "bench-results/scaling-rg24-run1.json"
just probe "data/lineitem-rg250k.parquet" "bench-results/row-groups-24-run1.json"
```

**And the two instruments spec 016 added**, one per file, three campaigns each:

```bash
just probe-cost       "data/lineitem.parquet" \
    "bench-results/row-group-cost-6-run1.json"
just probe-batch-size "data/lineitem.parquet" \
    "bench-results/batch-size-run1.json"
```

`probe-cost` is the per-row-group cost probe: the crate's own read of each column, the engine's
scan, the page shape of every row group, and the ladder of reader batch sizes. `probe-batch-size`
sweeps the reader's batch **limit** over the two shapes the lazy path serves and A/Bs the batch
the tree used against the batch it derives now. The `row-group-cost-*` reports are the source of
the ladder and the attribution in [§3j](#3j-the-row-group-count-and-what-it-costs), and the
`batch-size-*` reports are the source of the two-shape table in
[§3e](#3e-the-scans-decomposition-and-what-the-compaction-was-worth).

Three campaigns of each, interleaved, and then `python3 scripts/medians.py` derives both tables.
The Polars driver needs an interpreter with `polars` in it: set `PYTHON_BIN` if `python3` is not
the one that has it.

The comparison harness measures the load and the query separately, takes the **median** of the
timed runs, and **cross-checks every answer against the engine's own**. The `SUM` over 555,594
rows differs in the last bits between systems — floating-point addition is not associative and
the engines sum in different orders — so the check uses a relative tolerance rather than exact
equality.

To publish a table, run `just compare` at least three times, keep the reports, and derive the
medians with `scripts/medians.py`. The three reports behind the table in §2 are committed as
`bench-results/compare-run1.json`, `compare-run2.json` and `compare-run3.json`.

**Four more sets of reports sit beside them**, and every figure this file publishes is derived from
one of the five:

```bash
# the set this file publishes now
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-run*.json

# the set before it -- the code the increment replaced, on this same host, back to back
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-before-run*.json

# the set this one replaces
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-spec026-run*.json

# the set before that one
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-spec024-run*.json

# the set the code before spec 026 shipped was measured from
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-spec025-run*.json
```

**And the re-measurement of §2i sits beside them too**, under its own names: the code this increment
ships, and the code it replaces, measured back to back on this host.

```bash
# this increment's set
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-spec034-run*.json

# the code this increment replaces, on this same host, back to back
python3 scripts/medians.py bench-results/compare-spec034-before-run*.json
```

The third is what makes the published **old-and-new** figures checkable rather than typed, and the
guard checks all of them:

```bash
python3 scripts/check-published-figures.py
```

And the instrument that took the hot paths apart has its own two reports — the old code and the new
code, measured back to back on the same host:

```bash
cargo run --release -p columnar-engine --example operator_probe -- \
    --data data/lineitem.parquet --build data/orders.parquet \
    --out bench-results/operator-probe-after.json
```

**And the instrument that took the query apart** — the whole query, the plan output's own stage table,
and the time between them — is spec 024's, and it has two sets of reports as well:

```bash
just probe-query "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/query-cost-run1.json"

just probe-sort "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/sort-cost-run1.json"

just probe-join  "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-run1.json"
```

**And the join probe's re-measurement of §3p has its own two sets**, under their own names, and
`scripts/medians.py` prints them beside the others:

```bash
# this increment's set
just probe-join "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-spec034-run1.json"

# the code this increment replaces, on this same host, back to back
just probe-join "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-spec034-before-run1.json"
```

**And §3q's ladder is printed by the same script**, over the set this file publishes and the set spec
034 measured beside it — the leaves' drifts, their movements, the reconciliation and the cost's own
share — and every figure it prints is checked, inside the section that publishes it, by the guard:

```bash
python3 scripts/medians.py
python3 scripts/check-published-figures.py
```

**And §3r's file-state floor has its own three sets**, under their own names, because the instrument
changed — the build side's read and the machine's own measure are measured **in the iteration** now:

```bash
# this increment's set
just probe-join "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-spec036-run1.json"

# the code this increment replaces, on this same host, back to back
just probe-join "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-spec036-before-run1.json"

# the hunt: nine more campaigns of the same instrument
just probe-join "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/join-cost-spec036-hunt-run1.json"
```

**And the write-side probe**, in the two batch sizes, three campaigns each:

```bash
just probe-write "data/lineitem.parquet" \
    "bench-results/write-cost-run1.json" 65536
just probe-write "data/lineitem.parquet" \
    "bench-results/write-cost-engine-batch-run1.json" 125000
```

The probe writes the published file's own rows back out, commits the file the way an append-only store
commits it, reads it back through the engine's own reader, and compares it with the original byte for
byte. The third argument is the batch size the rows arrive in, and it is the variable: `65536` reproduces
the published file exactly, and the engine's own `125000` does not. The tables in
[§3s](#3s-the-write-side) are derived from those reports by `python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the guard
checks them like the rest.

**And the store's own probe**, three campaigns:

```bash
just probe-store "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/store-write-run1.json"
```

The probe writes the published file's own rows **through the store** -- the writer, the manifest and the
commit -- and compares the file the store wrote with the published file **byte for byte**. The tables in
[§3t](#3t-the-stores-own-write) are derived from those reports by `python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the
guard checks them like the rest.

**And the file boundary's probe**, three campaigns:

```bash
just probe-many-files "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/many-files-run1.json"
```

The probe writes the published file's own **six row groups** as six files, and reads the same rows **as
one file and as six**, through the engine's own reader, in the same campaign. The tables in
[§3u](#3u-the-file-boundarys-cost) are derived from those reports by `python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the
guard checks them like the rest.

**And the source's probe**, three campaigns:

```bash
just probe-file-set "data/lineitem.parquet" "bench-results/file-set-run1.json"
```

The probe writes the published file's own **six row groups** as six files, and reads the same rows **three
ways**: as the one file, as the six files one by one, and as the six files **read as one table** through the
engine's new source. The tables in [§3v](#3v-the-source-over-a-set-of-files) are derived from those reports
by `python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the guard checks them like the rest.

The probe runs the engine's **own** plans through the engine's own entry point and checks the answers
against the published ones before it times anything, so it is the same query the tables above publish.
Its report is **per query**: each query keeps its own shapes, its own whole-query time and its own
stage table. The tables in
[§3m](#3m-the-time-the-plan-output-does-not-name) are derived from those reports by
`python3 scripts/medians.py`, and the guard checks them like the rest.

**The plan output is in the campaigns too.** Every campaign records the plan output of every query it
ran — the stage breakdown, the rows each stage was handed and produced, and the memory the engine
estimated — so the stage figures in [§2h](#2h-the-hot-paths-taken-apart) are derived from the
committed reports rather than typed into the prose, and the guard checks them like the rest.

---

## 7. How to read this file

The framing rule for this project is **"kernel X on query Y"**, never "this database versus
DuckDB". A number here is a statement about *one operation on one query on one machine*, and it
is only meaningful next to its conditions.

So: the comparison kernel on the Q6 predicate set is 1.48× faster than the compiler's own
vectorisation of the same loop, and 2.44× faster than a genuinely scalar reference, on an M4 Max
with NEON, single-threaded, over 20 million rows. The engine's pushdown on the Q6 predicate set
costs 4.0 ms over twelve million rows. The engine's lazy scan of the same file, reading the two
columns Q6 needs and pushing the predicates down into the scan, takes **16.6 ms** at one thread —
against **32.0 ms** for DuckDB and **190.0 ms** for Polars — and at six workers it takes **3.9 ms**
against DuckDB's **8.0 ms**. The lazy path is **1.9× faster than DuckDB** at one thread and 11.5×
faster than Polars, and the reason it was not faster before is measured and named: the pushdown's mask was
handed to the reader as a row filter, and a row filter is a list of 2,775,138 row ranges for
2,178,861 surviving rows, applied one range at a time. The engine keeps the mask now, and the
scan that uses it copies each surviving row **once**.

**And the defect spec 016 found is the per-row-group cost.** The engine's reader used a
fixed batch of 65,536 rows, and that number divides none of the three files' row-group sizes — so
every row group ended on a **partial** batch, and a partial batch costs 0.67 ms. The engine paid
a per-row-group cost that DuckDB, Polars and the DataFusion peer did not pay, and the file with
the most parallelism was the slowest one. The batch size is derived from the file now, and the
engine's one-thread figure is flat across the three files — **13.2, 13.0 and 13.7 ms**, where the
set the bump replaces had **15.4, 15.7 and 16.4 ms**. See [§3j](#3j-the-row-group-count-and-what-it-costs).

**And the defect spec 020 found is the join's hasher.** The join's probe hashed with `SipHash`,
which is built to be resistant to collision attacks — a property a query engine's hash table has no
use for, and a cost it does, because the probe runs once for every row of the probe side. The join
stage went from **219.9 ms** to **117.7 ms** and the whole lazy query from **318.9 ms** to
**199.5 ms**, on the same file, in the same session, with the same answer. See
[§4](#4-the-defects-the-increments-found) and
[ADR-0027](docs/adr/0027-the-engine-joins-and-sorts.md).

**And the defect spec 021 found is the shape of the code, not a missing optimisation.** The join's
table was a `HashMap` over a 32-byte enum where an integer key wanted an array; the key reader
consulted a validity bitmap that carried no information; the sort built an enum per row where a
number would do. The engine's cold `join-orders` is **87.8 ms** — a hash join, with `orders` as the
build side, read inside the clock — against DuckDB's **89.0 ms** on the same two files; the engine's
cold `sort-topk` is **39.2 ms** against DuckDB's **37.0 ms**. The engine is **1.2 ms ahead** of
DuckDB on the join and **1.06× behind** on the sort, and ahead of Polars on both — by **2.5×** on the
join and by **33×** on the sort. The instrument that found it is committed, every candidate it measures is
checked against the engine's own output row for row before it is timed, and the code's share of the
numbers is measured against the old code on the same host. See
[§2h](#2h-the-hot-paths-taken-apart) and
[ADR-0028](docs/adr/0028-the-hot-paths-taken-apart.md).

**And the defect spec 024 found is an account with a hole in it.** The plan output is the engine's
own account of its work, and on the two queries that name a **join** and a **sort** it left **4.2 ms**
of each query **outside every stage** — the filter's selection expanded into six million row
numbers, 24 MB, before either stage's clock started. A selection that holds every row is **the
range** now, the expansion is **gone rather than renamed**, and the plan output's account closes to
**0.01 ms**. The instrument that found it is committed, it runs the engine's own plans, and it checks
each answer against the published one before it times anything. See
[§3m](#3m-the-time-the-plan-output-does-not-name) and
[ADR-0031](docs/adr/0031-the-time-the-plan-output-does-not-name.md).

And the eager read — the file → queryable load, which was the largest number in the repository —
is now **546.8 ms** against DuckDB's **1228.0 ms** on the same file at the same thread count. It is the one measurement in this file that is about the *file* rather
than the *query*, and it is swept like the other two. See [§2g](#2g-the-load-sweep--where-the-engines-biggest-number-was).
**Every absolute figure in this paragraph is this set's**, and this set was measured on a host
that was busy at the start and quieter at the end — see
[The conditions](#the-conditions). The ratios are
what the file claims, and they hold.
### 3ag. The width statement, paid

**What this section is**: **what a row costs**, measured against — and **what the estimate now charges**.

[§3af](#3af-the-batch-sizes-price) priced the reader's batch size and decided the rule's shape: **a byte budget**.
**And a byte budget has to know what a row costs before the read**, and there was no honest statement of that to
divide it by.

**The engine charged a `utf8` column 24 bytes a row** — a `String` header — **and a dictionary-encoded column's real
cost is its index's four**, which the estimate's own document said: *"the dictionary is shared by the whole column,
and the per-row cost is the index."* **Both paths did it**: the eager path had the chunk's encoding in hand and
charged the header anyway, and the lazy path could not see the encoding at all.

**And the file's own footer is not the statement either.** A column chunk's `total_uncompressed_size` over its
`num_values` is the **file's** bytes a row, and it is **the file's pages and not the working set**: the sixteen
columns come to **70.70** bytes a row that way against the **173.88** the allocator measured, and the file stores
`l_discount` in **0.50** bytes a row where the engine holds it in **eight**. **So the footer cannot be the
statement, the schema's type cannot be the statement, and the estimate was not the statement.**

#### The statement

**One statement, in one place, and the estimate and the reader's batch rule both read it.**

| the shape the row takes | the row holds | the bytes a row |
|---|---|---|
| a fixed-width value — an `int64` or a `float64` | the value itself | **8** |
| a dictionary's **index** | an `i32` into the column's dictionary | **4** |
| a plain string's `String` header | a `String` | **24** |

**And the value's own bytes ride on top**: a plain string's heap holds the string, a dictionary holds its distinct
values **once** for the whole column, and a number's eight bytes **are** its value.

**And the signal is the schema's own Arrow type, and not the chunk's encodings.** A chunk's `encodings` list names
`RLE_DICTIONARY` for `l_orderkey` — a column that is not dictionary-encoded at all. A `Dictionary(_, Utf8)` field is
a column of **indices** and a `Utf8` field is a column of **headers**, and it is the same signal the reader's own
arrays answer to.

#### The check

**The statement is checked against the allocator's measurement**, at the three widths of
[§3af](#3af-the-batch-sizes-price), **and the file's own footer is published beside both** — because it is the
file's pages, and the difference is the point.

| the shape | the columns the reader fetches | the statement | the file's own footer | the measurement | the difference |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **the two predicate columns** | 2 | **13.79** | **2.04** | **13.39** | **−0.40** |
| **the two materialised columns** | 2 | **16.25** | **16.53** | **16.03** | **−0.22** |
| **the file's sixteen columns** | 16 | **159.02** | **70.70** | **173.88** | **+14.86** |

**All in bytes a row.** **The statement is within 3% on the two narrow shapes and 9% low on the wide one.** **And the
file's own footer is 2.46× low** on the wide one — **70.70** bytes a row against the **173.88** the allocator
measured — and **6.56× low** on the narrow one. **On the two materialised columns the footer is within 3%**, and
that is an accident: the file stores those two columns the way the engine holds them.

**And the wide shape's difference is the plain strings.** **+14.86** bytes a row is the statement being **9.3% low**,
and the reason is that **the plain string's bytes are held twice in the batch** — once in Arrow's buffer and once in
the engine's own copy — **and the statement counts them once**. The dictionary path **borrows** the batch's values;
the plain path **copies** every string out of the batch, one allocation a row. **That is the wide shape's gap.**

#### And the estimate charges the batch

**The estimate now holds the file's rows for the columns the dataset holds, and the batch's rows for the columns the
reader fetches** — and **so the budget's verdict on a plan depends on the reader's batch size**, which it did not
before.

| the shape | the batch size | the read's peak | the estimate | the gap |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **the two predicate columns** | 62,500 | **1,166,672** | **2,361,802** | −1,195,130 |
| | 100,000 | **1,723,920** | **2,878,872** | −1,154,952 |
| | 125,000 | **2,169,536** | **3,223,586** | −1,054,050 |
| | 250,000 | **4,099,420** | **4,947,156** | −847,736 |
| | **1,000,000 — the engine's own** | **13,719,522** | **15,288,577** | −1,569,055 |
| **the two materialised columns** | 62,500 | **132,262,351** | **100,015,642** | +32,246,709 |
| | 100,000 | **107,268,751** | **100,625,016** | +6,643,735 |
| | 125,000 | **133,241,851** | **101,031,266** | +32,210,585 |
| | 250,000 | **135,405,827** | **103,062,516** | +32,343,311 |
| | **1,000,000 — the engine's own** | **147,294,211** | **115,250,016** | +32,044,195 |
| **the file's sixteen columns** | 62,500 | **1,144,709,903** | **965,574,092** | +179,135,811 |
| | **100,000 — the engine's own** | **1,012,041,759** | **971,537,430** | +40,504,329 |
| | 125,000 | **1,157,291,503** | **975,512,992** | +181,778,511 |
| | 250,000 | **1,183,960,455** | **995,390,808** | +188,569,647 |
| | 1,000,000 | **1,307,720,215** | **1,114,657,705** | +193,062,510 |

**The gap is the read's peak less the estimate** — a negative gap is the estimate sitting above the read — and
**the peak is the new campaign's**: the reports hold it, and the guard checks it inside this section.

**And the estimate was one number a shape before, and it was wrong in both directions.** It said **1,500,016** bytes
on the two predicate columns, **99,000,016** on the two materialised ones, and **1,549,500,016** on the sixteen —
**and it did not depend on the batch size at all**, so the budget could not see the knob's price. **Now it does**:
on the narrow shape the estimate says **15,288,577** bytes where the read holds **13,719,522** — **1.11× high** at a
whole row group where it was **9.15× low** (**1,500,016** against **13,719,490**) — and on the wide one it says
**971,537,430** where the read holds **1,012,041,759** — **4% low** where it was **25% high** (**1,549,500,016**
against **1,157,291,375**). **And the reads moved with the estimates**: the campaign was taken again after the
engine changed, and the read's peak moved by **32** bytes on the narrow shape and **128** on the wide one — so the
figures here are the new campaign's, and the old ones stand beside them.

**And the wide shape is still refused by the default budget**, at every batch size, and now for a reason that is not
an accident: **the plan does not fit**. The estimate says **971,537,430** bytes at the engine's own batch size and
the read holds **1,012,041,759** there — and the budget is **256 MiB**.

**And the estimate is close, and not an upper bound.** The **statement** is what a row costs in the engine's own
storage, and the engine's own containers hold **spare capacity** and each string is **a separate allocation** — so
the estimate runs **4% low** against the wide shape's read. **The measurement is published here so that the
difference is visible**, and the guard checks the figures in this section against the committed reports.

#### What this changes

**The estimate and the reader's batch rule now share one statement**, so the rule can divide a byte budget by what a
row costs. **And the default has moved**: [§3ah](#3ah-the-default-moved) publishes **every** figure the move touched
**old and new**, re-measured against the old code in the same session.

**And the open question this leaves behind** is the plain string's copy: **the conversion copies every string out of
the Arrow batch, one allocation a row, and the dictionary path does not**. **That is the wide shape's gap, and it is
not fixed here** — the fix is a change to the conversion, and it wants its own measurement.

Every figure in this section is derived from the committed reports by `scripts/medians.py` and checked by the guard
**inside this section**. The reports are `bench-results/batch-width-run1.json` and its two siblings, and
`bench-results/batch-memory-run1.json` and its two siblings. The instruments are
`crates/columnar-engine/examples/batch_width_probe.rs` and
`crates/columnar-engine/examples/batch_memory_probe.rs`. The decision is in
[ADR-0062](docs/adr/0062-the-width-statement.md).

**The conditions**: three campaigns of three repetitions, on one host, with the load average between **6.46** and
**6.91**. **The memory is measured with a counting allocator**, and the time is not measured here — a time measured
beside a counting allocator is not the engine's time.

---

### 3ah. The default moved

**What this section is**: the headline figure of every family this increment touched, **old and new, side by side** --
and **the rule that moved**, and the batch size it gives each width.

**What moved, and why, in four sentences.**

1. **The default was a row limit of 131,072 rows**, and the batch it gave was the largest divisor of a row group's
   row count under it -- **125,000 rows**, whatever the read fetched.
2. **It is a byte budget of 16 MiB now**, and the batch is the largest divisor of the row group's row count whose
   bytes fit it -- so **the batch depends on the width the read fetches**.
3. **And the budget is the read's, and the workers share it**, so a read on *n* workers gives each of them an
   *n*-th of it.
4. **And the rate the budget is divided by is in eighths of a byte**, because a column's rows carry a **validity
   bit** and a bit is an eighth of a byte: a rate in whole bytes charges the bit a whole byte, which is **eight
   times the truth**, and the rule then gives every shape less than the budget allows.

**And the rule's own output**, from the width probe -- the rule's output is a figure, and a figure with no
instrument behind it is a claim:

| the width | the columns the reader fetches | at one worker | at two | at four | at six | at fourteen |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| the two predicate columns | 2 | **1,000,000** | 500,000 | 250,000 | 200,000 | 62,500 |
| the two materialised columns | 2 | **1,000,000** | 500,000 | 250,000 | 125,000 | 62,500 |
| the file's sixteen columns | 16 | **100,000** | 50,000 | 25,000 | 15,625 | 6,250 |

**The two narrow shapes get the whole row group at one worker**, because a whole row group of either of them fits
the budget: **13.79 MB** and **16.25 MB** against the budget's **16.78 MB**. **And the wide shape gets 100,000
rows**, because its statement is **159.02 bytes a row** and a whole row group of it -- **159.02 MB** -- does not fit.

**And every headline figure that moved**, old and new. **The left column is the old code**, measured **back to back
with the new one** on this host, three campaigns each, **interleaved** -- so the difference between the two is the
**code's** and not the machine's:

| the family | the figure | the code the default replaces | this file publishes |
|---|---|---|---|
| the comparison | the engine's cold `q6`, lazy | 13.9 | **16.6** |
| the comparison | `DuckDB`'s cold `q6` -- **the yardstick** | 32.0 | **32.0** |
| the query probe | the whole query, cold | 88.101 | **87.843** |
| the sort probe | the stage, cold | 1.52 | **2.34** |
| the join probe | the stage, cold | 20.736 | **20.863** |
| the join probe | the floor the probe -- **the machine's own measure** | 4.662 | **4.735** |
| the write probe | the write of the whole file | 1,629.665 | **1,641.539** |
| the store's probe | the commit, all five steps | 23.651 | **27.797** |
| the materialised column | Arrow's decode | 4.21 | **4.26** |
| the dictionary probe | the crate's read, expanded | 13.56 | **13.81** |
| the row-group probe | the derived batch | 13.59 | **14.20** |
| the sweep | the engine at 1 thread | 13.1 | **13.2** |
| the curve | the engine at 6M rows | 53.9 | **53.1** |

**And the engine's cold `q6` got slower, and the yardstick did not.** The lazy `q6` is **16.6 ms** where it was
**13.9 ms** -- **2.7 ms slower, 19%** -- and `DuckDB`'s is **32.0 ms** in both sets, and the machine's own floor --
the join probe's floor, which has no engine in it -- is **4.735 ms** against **4.662 ms**, **1.6%** apart. **So the
movement is the code's and not the host's.** The whole query the file publishes is the other way: the join-orders
query, cold, is **87.843 ms** where it was **88.101 ms**.

**And the scan's curve says where the 2.7 ms is.** The `lazy_batch_size` probe walks the batch over the **real
Q6's predicate set** -- `l_discount` and the dictionary `l_shipdate` -- and the scan's cost by batch is:

| the batch | 62,500 | 125,000 | 250,000 | 1,000,000 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **the real Q6's predicate set** | 14.902 ms | 14.141 ms | **13.740 ms** | 14.878 ms |
| one column, one predicate | 12.463 ms | 12.396 ms | 12.444 ms | 12.447 ms |

**The predicate set's curve turns up at a whole row group**: the minimum is at **250,000 rows**, and the rule's
output at one worker is **1,000,000**, which is **8.3%** above it. **And the one-column shape does not care** --
its curve is flat to within **0.5%** over the same range, and the rule's output is within **0.4%** of its best
point. **So the shape decides, and the rule cannot see the shape**: the two shapes have the same width, **16
bytes a row**, and the same batch, and one of them wants a quarter of a row group and the other does not.

**And the sweep's three shapes are flat**, over the whole range from 62,500 rows to a whole row group -- the filter's
scan moves **0.120 ms**, the join's part **0.208 ms** and the wide read **8.505 ms**, and each is inside its own
spread, which runs from **1.022 ms** to **24.070 ms**. **So the turn-up is the predicate set's, and not the batch
size's price in general.**

**And the budget's number is an open question, and it is published here rather than settled.** **16 MiB** was
chosen by a measurement from both sides -- above the narrow shapes' whole row group (**15.3 MiB**) and at or below
the wide shape's next batch (**20.7 MiB**, **125,000 rows**) -- and the rule's output is what that number produces,
per width and per worker count, in the table above. **A smaller budget would move the narrow shapes to the predicate
set's optimum and the wide shape below anything measured**: at **4 MiB** the wide shape's batch would be **25,000
rows**, and the sweep's lowest rung is **62,500**. **And the scan's curve was about to change anyway**: the next
increment was to make the reader own the Parquet decode, and the decode is what the batch's optimum turns on. **So the
number is not changed on a guess here.** It is named, measured, and left open: **the byte budget's number, once the
reader owns the decode.** **And the reader does not own it** -- spec 052 measured the shape and found it **4.957 ms
slower** than the engine's own scan, so the decode is not going to move and **the number is settled**, at **16 MiB**
([§3ai](#3ai-the-reader-owns-the-decode)).

**And the set the file published before** -- measured on an earlier day, on the tree at spec 050's close -- is kept
under its own name, `bench-results/<family>-spec050-run<n>.json`, so the figure the file published is still
derivable from a committed report. **And the old code's figures are in their own sets**,
`bench-results/<family>-spec051-before-run<n>.json`, measured in the same session and interleaved with the new
code's, so the difference in the table above is the code's.

**And one family is the exception, and it says so.** The sweep's own set is the only one whose "before" is the set
this file replaces, because the sweep's instrument grew a fifth point and the campaigns were taken on different
days. **So the sweep's difference is attributed by measurement rather than assumed**: the same instrument, the two
codes, back to back on this host, three campaigns each, puts the code's share under **0.2 ms** on the join's part --
and the set this file replaces reads **25.038 ms** for that part where the old code reads **17.801 ms** today. **So
the movement between the two published sets is the host's.** The attribution's reports are
`bench-results/batch-sweep-ab-oldcode-run<n>.json` and `bench-results/batch-sweep-ab-newcode-run<n>.json`, and it is
published in [§3af](#3af-the-batch-sizes-price).

Every figure in this section is derived from the committed reports by `scripts/medians.py` and checked by the guard
**inside this section**. The reports are `bench-results/compare-run1.json` and its two siblings,
`bench-results/batch-width-run1.json` and its two siblings, and `bench-results/batch-size-run1.json` and its two
siblings. The instruments are `crates/columnar-engine/examples/batch_width_probe.rs`,
`crates/columnar-engine/examples/lazy_batch_size.rs` and `crates/columnar-engine/examples/batch_sweep_probe.rs`.
The decision is in [ADR-0063](docs/adr/0063-the-default-moves-to-the-byte-budget.md) and the rule's rate is in
[§3ag](#3ag-the-width-statement-paid).

**The conditions**: the old code and the new one were measured **back to back**, three campaigns each,
**interleaved**, on one host, at a load average between **1.88** and **14.85** -- the width, sweep and memory
campaigns were the busiest, and the load average is in every report.

---

### 3ai. The reader owns the decode

**The house promised the reader, and the measurement says no.** [§3ah](#3ah-the-default-moved) said that *the scan's
curve is about to change anyway, because the next increment makes the reader own the Parquet decode*, and it left **the
byte budget's number** open until that happens. **And the promise was made on the strength of a decision taken before the
code it describes existed**: [ADR-0018](docs/adr/0018-the-scan-reads-the-parquet-decode.md) prototyped the rewrite and
**refused** it — *the scan's read is the Parquet decode, and owning it is not worth it*.

**And the refusal was stale.** It was taken on **2026-10-26**, against a tree that no longer exists, and **three things
moved since**, each of which touches the shape it measured: the `parquet` crate bumped from 54 to **58.4.1**; the
default's rule moved from a row limit to a **byte budget**, so the engine's own batch for the narrow shapes is now a
**whole row group** — and the old probe never read one; and the pushdown gained a **dictionary path** the old shape did
not have. **So the shape is measured again, on the code that is here now, at the batch sizes the rule now produces.**

**And the shape that could win is measured too** — the one ADR-0018 did not have: **the engine's own reader**, with the
page walk, the dictionary and the indices, and **no expansion at all**. The predicate's column is dictionary-encoded, so
the reader compares the **dictionary** — eleven values — once, and the rows are selected **by the indices**. The expansion
of six million floats never happens.

**Six shapes**, on the same file, in the same process, warm, three campaigns each:

| the shape | the batch | the median |
|---|---|---|
| **A** — the parquet crate's own decode of the two columns | 1,000,000 | 8.764 ms |
| **B** — Arrow's decode of the same two columns | 1,000,000 | 7.490 ms |
| **C** — A, plus the predicate over the decoded buffer, plus the compaction | 1,000,000 | 13.108 ms |
| **D** — the engine's own scan, as it is today | 1,000,000 | 12.291 ms |

**And D at every batch size the rule produces**, beside the shapes:

| the batch | A — the crate | B — Arrow | C — the old shape | D — the engine |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1,000,000 — the rule's own at one worker** | 8.764 ms | 7.490 ms | 13.108 ms | 12.291 ms |
| 500,000 | 8.245 ms | 7.369 ms | 12.987 ms | 12.459 ms |
| 250,000 | 8.226 ms | 7.457 ms | 12.844 ms | 12.252 ms |
| 62,500 | 8.423 ms | 7.352 ms | 13.191 ms | 12.417 ms |
| 16,384 | 8.891 ms | 7.585 ms | 14.013 ms | 12.895 ms |
| 65,536 | 8.448 ms | 7.531 ms | 13.232 ms | 12.289 ms |
| 4,096 | 8.793 ms | 8.576 ms | 13.210 ms | 13.968 ms |

**And the reader's own shapes**, against the engine's own scan at its own default:

| the shape | the median | against the engine's scan |
|---|---|---|
| **D** — the engine's own scan | **12.435 ms** | *the reference* |
| **E3** — the engine's own reader, **row group by row group** | **17.392 ms** | **+4.957 ms** |
| **E4** — the same, **with the expansion** | **18.500 ms** | +6.244 ms |
| **E** — the engine's own reader, **the whole file's** | **17.812 ms** | +5.529 ms |
| **E2** — the same, **with the expansion** | **19.202 ms** | +6.633 ms |

**And every difference is the median of the campaigns' own paired differences**, not the difference of two medians: the
two sides are measured in the same campaign, so their difference is the shape's and not the machine's.

**So the reader is not worth building, and the reason is priced.** The engine's own reader is **4.957 ms slower** than
the engine's own scan, and the idea it carries — **never expanding the predicate's column** — is worth **1.141 ms**
inside it (E4 − E3). **The shape's own handicap is 0.453 ms** (E − E3): the whole file's mask, and the file read in
two passes instead of one. **So the reader costs more than three times the prize it carries.**

**And the mechanism is named.** The parquet crate's decode is **one pass**: it reads the indices and gathers the values
in the same loop, and hands the engine an expanded array. **The engine's own reader is three passes**: the indices, the
dictionary's answer mapped through them, and then the survivors gathered. **And the map is a scalar lookup a row where
the expansion is a vectorised comparison** — which is why the engine's own dictionary path exists for strings, and why
the house's own history says it *cost 41 ms before it was fixed*. **The expansion is not the expensive part; the map that
would avoid it is dearer.**

**And ADR-0018's refusal holds.** The old shape C is **13.108 ms** where the engine's own scan is **12.291 ms**, at the
batch the rule now produces — **0.840 ms** of Arrow's layer, paired over the campaigns, the old ADR measured, on a different day and a
different crate. **So the decode is the parquet crate's, and it is the floor.**

**And the instrument is checked against the reference it measures against.** Every shape's survivors are compared, **value
by value**, with the engine's own scan's — **2,178,861 rows** — and the probe stops if they disagree. **The engine's own
reader is not trusted to answer about itself.**

**And the budget's number is settled against this.** The byte budget's number was left open *until the reader owns the
decode*, and the decode **is not going to move**: the reader is **4.957 ms slower**, and the parquet crate's decode is
what the engine reads through. **So the number is not waiting for the reader — it is settled**, and it stays **16 MiB**.

Every figure in this section is derived from the committed reports by `scripts/medians.py` and checked by the guard
**inside this section**. The reports are `bench-results/own-decode-run1.json` and its two siblings. The instrument is
`crates/columnar-engine/examples/own_decode_probe.rs`, and the decision is in
[ADR-0064](docs/adr/0064-the-reader-does-not-own-the-decode.md).

**The conditions**: three campaigns, on one host, at a load average of **6.02**, and the load average is in every report.
